HTTP/2 and HTTP/3, IPv6 on.

#### Security headers

`apps/web/securityHeaders.ts` is the one definition. A viewer-response CloudFront Function adds it to every response of the app and the API; `vite preview` sends it too (`vite.config.ts`), so **the browser tests run the production bundle under it**, and `openPage` fails a test on any CSP violation the console reports. `pnpm dev` doesn't send it: React's dev runtime injects inline scripts.

| Header                       | Value                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                     |
| ---------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| `Content-Security-Policy`    | `default-src 'self'; script-src 'self' https://challenges.cloudflare.com; frame-src https://challenges.cloudflare.com; style-src 'self' 'unsafe-inline'; img-src 'self' data:; font-src 'self' data:; connect-src 'self'; object-src 'none'; base-uri 'none'; form-action 'self'; frame-ancestors 'none'` |
| `Strict-Transport-Security`  | `max-age=63072000; includeSubDomains` (users' subdomains on the shared domain serve no web)                                                                                                                                                                                                               |
| `X-Content-Type-Options`     | `nosniff`                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 |
| `Referrer-Policy`            | `no-referrer`                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                             |
| `X-Frame-Options`            | `DENY` (for browsers without `frame-ancestors`)                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                           |
| `Cross-Origin-Opener-Policy` | `same-origin`                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                             |

`'unsafe-inline'` for styles is deliberate and tested: the inbox message frame (§20.22.3) is a `srcdoc` document, which inherits the app's policy, and the sanitized mail's `style` attributes and the frame's own `<style>` are inline styles; so are `data:` images. A new browser test renders a styled HTML mail with an inline image and checks the computed style; it fails with `style-src 'self'` or `img-src 'self'` (tried). Scripts are limited to the app and Turnstile: `script-src` and `frame-src` name `https://challenges.cloudflare.com`, where Cloudflare serves the sign-up widget's script and challenge frame (§20.27; Cloudflare's CSP guidance asks for exactly these two). The browser tests put the Turnstile emulator's origin there instead (`securityHeaders(turnstileOrigin)`), so sign-up runs under the policy too; the emulator's own contract test, a probe page with inline scripts, opts out (`openPage({ isCspBypassed: true })`). The message frame still adds its own stricter policy (`default-src 'none'`). A CloudFront Function rather than a response headers policy because the flat-rate Free and Pro plans allow only managed policies, and AWS's managed `SecurityHeadersPolicy` has no CSP.

#### WAF

One web ACL (scope `CLOUDFRONT`), four rules, so the Free plan's five fit:

| Priority | Rule                                    | What                                                                                                                                                                               |
| -------- | --------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| 0        | `AWSManagedRulesAmazonIpReputationList` | Amazon threat intelligence: bots, scanners, known bad addresses                                                                                                                    |
| 1        | `AWSManagedRulesKnownBadInputsRuleSet`  | Log4Shell-style and other exploit payloads, exploitable paths                                                                                                                      |
| 2        | `auth-rate`                             | 300 requests per IP in 5 minutes on `/v1/auth/`, `/v1/oauth/`, `/v1/device/` (sign-up, login, OAuth, device login), blocked above; `alias login` alone polls 60 times in 5 minutes |
| 3        | `flood-rate`                            | 3,000 requests per IP in 5 minutes on everything but `/img/*`                                                                                                                      |

These are a second layer: the app's own limits (token failures, device requests, OAuth registration, MCP, and §20.27's sign-up and login challenges and accounts per client) stay the precise ones; all of them count the address `ClientSource` vouches for, so behind CloudFront the viewer. **Left out on purpose:** the core rule set (`AWSManagedRulesCommonRuleSet`: it blocks bodies over 8 KB, HTML and URLs in bodies, `169.254.169.254` in bodies, URI paths over 1 KB and requests without `User-Agent`, so drafts, OpenPGP keys, webhook URLs, long image proxy URLs and CLIs would trip it, while the API decodes every payload with a schema, has no SQL and renders no HTML server-side); the anonymous IP list (it blocks VPNs, Tor and hosting providers, which privacy-minded users rely on); a rate rule on `/img/*` (Gmail and Apple Mail fetch images for all their users from a few addresses; SR-12's per-user key is the way to limit it). WAF rate rules count single IPv6 addresses, not /64s; the app's limits do the /64.

#### Cost: flat-rate plan or pay-as-you-go

| Option (list prices, October 2026) | Per month                                                                                                                                                                                                                                           | Fits                                                                                                                                |
| ---------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Pay-as-you-go                      | WAF: $5 web ACL + $1 × 4 rules + $0.60/M requests (≈ $9 at no traffic). CloudFront: always free up to 1 TB and 10 M requests, 2 M function invocations; then ≈ $0.085/GB and $0.012 per 10 k HTTPS requests in Europe, $0.10/M function invocations | Everything; the cheaper option above Pro's allowance (≈ $135 at 50 M requests and 1.5 TB, functions included, vs. Business at $200) |
| Flat-rate **Free**                 | **$0**: CloudFront, WAF (5 rules), DDoS, CloudFront Functions; 1 M requests and 100 GB allowance (blocked requests don't count)                                                                                                                     | Pre-launch and private beta: this configuration (5 behaviors, managed policies, functions)                                          |
| Flat-rate **Pro**                  | $15: 10 M requests, 50 TB, 25 WAF rules, 10 behaviors                                                                                                                                                                                               | Early beta to "growing" (§14.1): ≈ 3 M API requests plus the app and images                                                         |
| Flat-rate Business                 | $200: custom cache, origin request and response headers policies, 125 M requests                                                                                                                                                                    | Not needed: nothing here uses custom policies                                                                                       |

**Decision: start on the flat-rate Free plan, move to Pro when traffic passes about 1 M requests a month, and switch to pay-as-you-go once Pro's 10 M is outgrown.** The configuration was built for that path: one distribution, five behaviors, four WAF rules, only managed policies, functions associated with this distribution alone, no real-time logs, no custom rule groups; the same configuration runs unchanged on pay-as-you-go. A flat-rate plan never bills overage (sustained excess is answered by slower delivery, after warnings) and covers the WAF, which pay-as-you-go would bill at about $9 a month from day one. Caveats: the plan is chosen in the console or the PricingPlanManager API, which Alchemy doesn't model, so it is a manual step after the first deploy; AWS accounts on the AWS Free Tier plan can't subscribe (§14.1); a distribution on a plan must keep its web ACL and can't be deleted until the plan is cancelled; Lambda@Edge would be billed extra (none used).

#### Tests

- **Core:** `ClientAddress` (parsing, RFC 5952 output, /64 keys however the address is written), `OriginSecret` (current and retired keys accepted, nine near-misses refused). The move of the IP parser into `internal/ip.ts` found a bug: an IPv6 address written without `::` parsed to 18 bytes, because `Arr.replicate` never returns an empty array (harmless for the webhook guard's prefix checks, fixed for all).
- **Adapter (contract, AWS documentation values):** `FunctionUrl` behind CloudFront trusts the documented `198.51.100.10:46532` only with a valid secret, refuses six forgeries (no secret, a forged viewer address, a forged `x-alias-source-ip`, an unknown key version, the wrong secret, a bare secret), fails closed without a usable viewer address, and passes no front header on; `CloudFront.viewerAddress` reads IPv4, IPv6 bare and bracketed, mapped IPv4, and refuses missing or out-of-range ports and lists.
- **Emulator:** `tools/lambda-emulator` `CloudFrontServer` emulates the API's behavior in front of `FunctionUrlServer`, following the CloudFront developer guide: `Host` becomes the origin's, `CloudFront-Viewer-Address` is set from the viewer's socket (IPv6 bare), origin custom headers overwrite viewer copies, `X-Forwarded-For` is appended to, `X-Amz-Cf-Id` added, `X-Real-IP`, `X-Forwarded-Proto`, `X-Edge-*` and hop-by-hop headers removed. Its contract test runs the production adapter behind it over IPv4 and IPv6 (`::1`); the events decode with `FunctionUrlEvent` and the viewer header parses with the production parser. One behavior is an assumption: the docs don't say what CloudFront does with a viewer-sent `CloudFront-Viewer-Address`; the emulator replaces it (deploy check below).
- **API handler on fakecloud:** called directly, 20 bad tokens with a different forged `CloudFront-Viewer-Address`, `x-alias-source-ip`, `X-Forwarded-For` and origin secret each still lock the one `sourceIp`; twenty IPv6 addresses in one /64 lock the /64 and not the next; behind CloudFront, viewers on one edge address get separate limits (IPv4 and IPv6 viewers), and four ways around CloudFront get 403.
- **Stack (`e2e`):** the API on the whole emulated stack, origin secrets in fakecloud's SSM read through the production `FrontConfig`, behind two emulated edges (`127.0.0.1` and `::1`): served through both, 403 for three direct attempts, 20 bad tokens through the IPv4 edge, each claiming another address in every header, lock that viewer while the IPv6 viewer on the same function URL is not locked, and the same for sign-up and login challenges (§20.27's per-client limit, set to 5 here); a cross-site `Origin` through the edge gets §20.30's 403 before anything is counted.
- **Browser:** every test but the Turnstile emulator's probe runs under the production headers (sign-up with the widget included); a new one renders a styled HTML mail with an inline image in the inbox frame.
- **Infra:** typechecked only (`tsc -p infra`).

#### What a deploy must verify (needs an AWS account)

1. That a viewer-sent `CloudFront-Viewer-Address` is replaced, not passed on or joined (send one through the distribution; the API must key on the real address). The adapter refuses joined values, so the failure mode is a 403, not a spoofed key.
2. That `AllViewerExceptHostHeader` forwards `CloudFront-Viewer-Address` (else every API request is a logged 403), and its exact value for an IPv6 viewer (bare, port after the last colon).
3. That a direct call to the API's function URL gets 403, and that CloudFront's origin custom header reaches the Lambda as `x-alias-origin-secret` and overwrites a viewer-sent one.
4. OAC to S3 with the bucket policy's `AWS:SourceArn`, the SPA function's routing (`/oauth/authorize`, `/device?code=…` → `index.html`; `/assets/*` untouched), and `Cache-Control` honoured under `CachingOptimized` (`index.html` revalidated, assets a year).
5. The image proxy under `/img/*` with OAC and both Lambda permissions (moved from its own distribution), and that the security-headers function doesn't run there.
6. The security headers on the real origin (HSTS over HTTPS, CSP with WebAuthn, the OAuth consent page and the real Turnstile widget, whose script and frame come from `challenges.cloudflare.com`), and that every API path (including `POST` bodies with `Authorization`) works through `/v1/*`, `/mcp` and `/.well-known/*`.
7. WAF: the managed groups don't block real traffic (watch sampled requests a week, or start the groups in count mode), rate rules block at the set rates with their URI scope-down, and the Free plan accepts the ACL as configured (rate rules with scope-down statements; if not, Pro).
8. ACM: the certificate is issued through the Cloudflare CNAME the stack writes, within the deploy's wait (15 minutes), and renews unattended; the Cloudflare CNAME to the distribution at the apex coexists with the apex MX (§20.37).
9. Subscribing the distribution to the flat-rate Free plan (console), which also confirms the account is eligible, and the plan's notion of "one apex domain".
10. `Alchemy` details: the bucket policy bound after the distribution, `Website.AssetDeployment` uploading `apps/web/dist` (`pnpm infra:deploy` builds it), and the deploy reading `/alias/<stage>/origin-secrets` (the `Keyring` action creates it, `OriginSecretHeader` reads it after), with Alchemy's own credentials.
11. The Cloudflare side (§20.37): the zone lookup with the token's permissions, the Turnstile widget's keys reaching the API and SSM, the DNS records' names as Cloudflare stores them (ACM's record without its trailing dot, `*.<domain>` MX), the two ACM actions on a fresh certificate, and the quota request and budget being created.

**Residual risks.** The origin secret is in the distribution config and the Alchemy state (S3), so anyone with `cloudfront:GetDistributionConfig` or state access can call the function URL as CloudFront and name any address; it buys rate-limit evasion, not access (every endpoint still needs its credential). Rotate it with the other keyrings. Only the `local` stage runs without the certificate, on `*.cloudfront.net`, where passkeys (bound to the app domain) don't work, so nobody signs up there. The same-origin check (SR-14, §20.30) compares `Origin` with `ALIAS_WEB_ORIGIN`, `https://<app domain>`: that is the origin browsers see once the certificate is attached, since the app and the API share it and CloudFront forwards `Origin` and `Sec-Fetch-Site` unchanged (`AllViewerExceptHostHeader`). On the `*.cloudfront.net` name every change is refused, which is consistent with passkeys not working there.

### 20.27 Sign-up and login abuse protection (2026-10-23)

SR-9 (§20.25): `registerOptions`, `register` and `loginOptions` had no limit, and with attestation `none` a script with a software authenticator could register in a loop, squatting usernames (and their subdomains) and multiplying every per-user allowance. Now every passkey ceremony is counted against the client and the account, sign-up needs a solved Cloudflare Turnstile widget, a circuit breaker caps sign-ups for everyone, and role names can't be taken.

**Limits** (`apps/api/src/SignupLimits.ts`, counters in `SignupLimitRepo` on the `Quota` helper: one conditional `UpdateItem` each, fixed UTC windows, TTL a window after their own):

| What is counted                                                  | Per            | Window   | Default                            | Past it                                                           |
| ---------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------- | -------- | ---------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Sign-up and login challenges (`registerOptions`, `loginOptions`) | client address | UTC hour | 30                                 | `TooManyAttempts` (429), before anything is read or written       |
| Accounts created (`register`)                                    | client address | UTC day  | 5                                  | `TooManyAttempts` (429)                                           |
| Accounts created, everyone together (circuit breaker)            | the service    | UTC hour | 100 (`ALIAS_MAX_SIGNUPS_PER_HOUR`) | `SignupUnavailable` `paused` (503); logins carry on               |
| Failed passkey assertions                                        | account        | UTC hour | 10                                 | `TooManyAttempts` (429) for that account's logins, valid ones too |

Sign-up and login (`registerOptions`, `register`, `loginOptions`, `login`) also run the same-origin check that cookie-authenticated changes get (SR-14, §20.30: `Sec-Fetch-Site`/`Origin` must name the web app, a body must be JSON), before anything is counted, so another site can't make its visitors' browsers spend their address's challenge budget (`CrossSiteRequest`, 403). The client address is `ClientSource`, the hashed address the token, device and OAuth limits use; behind CloudFront it must be the viewer's, which §20.26 (SR-8) provides. `register` counts the client's daily limit before the global one, so a script that runs into its own limit can't also use up everyone's. Logins with discoverable passkeys name no username, so the per-account limit counts failed assertions (signature, challenge or origin mismatch, a `userHandle` naming another user) against the user whose passkey was presented; credential IDs are never handed out (no `allowCredentials`), so only someone holding one can lock its account, and only for the rest of the hour. The counter lives in the user's partition and goes with an erasure. Challenge storage is bounded per client: a challenge item lives 5 minutes (readers ignore expired ones, TTL deletes them), a client gets at most 30 an hour, a registration challenge only after a Turnstile pass, an erasure challenge only with a session.

**Turnstile** was chosen over reCAPTCHA (no ad-tracking business behind it; DNS is on Cloudflare already). `registerOptions` takes `{ username, turnstileToken }` and, in this order: counts the challenge, checks the username (invalid, reserved, taken), redeems the token, issues the challenge; a refused username doesn't use the token up. `GET /v1/auth/signup` names the widget (`siteKey`, `scriptUrl`). The client (`packages/turnstile`, `@alias/turnstile`) posts the secret and token to Siteverify as a form, 5 s per attempt, retrying twice with the same `idempotency_key` on a network error, a timeout, a 5xx or `internal-error`. `missing-input-response`, `invalid-input-response` and `timeout-or-duplicate` mean the visitor must solve the widget again (`HumanCheckFailed`, 400); every other failure, including a wrong secret, is ours or Cloudflare's, and sign-up fails closed (`SignupUnavailable` `human-check-unavailable`, 503, logged). An empty token or one over the documented 2,048 characters is refused without a call. We send no `remoteip`: the widget already talks to Cloudflare from the browser, and the address adds nothing we need. `hostname` and `action` aren't checked: Cloudflare's own test keys answer `example.com` and no action (recorded), and the widget's hostname list in the Cloudflare dashboard already ties it to the app's domain. The secret key is an SSM `SecureString` (`/alias/<stage>/turnstile-secret`, named by `ALIAS_TURNSTILE_SECRET_PARAMETER`, read at cold start like §20.14's keys through `Parameters.secretConfig`, `ssm:GetParameter` on that one ARN); the site key is public (`ALIAS_TURNSTILE_SITE_KEY`; since §20.37 the stack creates the widget on Cloudflare and sets both, the `local` stage uses the always-passing test keys); `ALIAS_TURNSTILE_URL` points both at the emulator in emulated runs.

**The emulator** (`tools/turnstile-emulator`) serves `POST /turnstile/v0/siteverify` and `GET /turnstile/v0/api.js` for the documented test keys. To verify it, the real Siteverify endpoint was recorded with the test secrets (no account needed): ten exchanges in `packages/turnstile/fixtures/recorded-*.json`, next to the four documented bodies. The production schema decodes all of them, and the emulator's contract test replays each request and gets the same status and body (only `challenge_ts` differs). The recordings contradict the docs in places, and the emulator follows the recordings: a test secret accepts any token, not only the dummy one; successes carry `hostname: "example.com"`, no `action`, and `metadata.result_with_testing_key`; a missing or unknown secret and a malformed body are 400, a `GET` 405. The widget script was recorded the same way: Cloudflare's `api.js` in headless Chromium with each test site key (the passing keys call `callback` with `XXXX.DUMMY.TOKEN.XXXX` and fill a hidden `cf-turnstile-response` input; the blocking keys call `error-callback` with `600010`; invisible keys take no space), and `browser/test/turnstile.test.ts` runs the same probe against the emulator's script. `pnpm dev` starts it on the worktree's port block (`ALIAS_TURNSTILE_PORT`), and the API verifies against it with the always-passing test secret.

**Web.** The sign-up form renders the widget explicitly (`action: "signup"`, dark theme) from the script URL the API names, keeps "Create passkey" disabled until a token arrives, and mounts a new widget for every attempt, since a token is redeemed once. The widget is a class component so it is rendered once per mount and removed with its container (a stale widget would still call back when its token expires). The errors read "The human check expired", "Too many attempts. Please try again in an hour." and "Sign-ups are paused for a while". **CSP for the CloudFront change** (§20.26, SR-8): Cloudflare's documented directives, `script-src https://challenges.cloudflare.com` and `frame-src https://challenges.cloudflare.com`, next to `'self'`; `connect-src 'self'` is enough (no pre-clearance). `api.js` must be loaded from Cloudflare's URL, never proxied or self-hosted.

**Reserved names.** Usernames already reserved infrastructure, role and brand names by their confusable skeleton (`Username.ts`); the missing RFC 2142 mailboxes (`marketing`, `noc`, `usenet`, `uucp`) are added. Local parts on a user's subdomain can no longer be role mailboxes (`Address.isRoleLocal`): RFC 2142's (`info`, `marketing`, `sales`, `support`, `abuse`, `noc`, `security`, `postmaster`, `hostmaster`, `usenet`, `news`, `webmaster`, `www`, `uucp`, `ftp`), `mailer-daemon`, `root`, and the addresses certificate authorities mail to prove control of a domain (`admin`, `administrator`, plus the RFC 2142 ones; CA/Browser Forum Baseline Requirements §3.2.2.4.4), in any case and with any `+detail`. Creating one through the API fails with `InvalidAlias` `role-address`; mail to one is never turned into an alias on the fly (dropped as `unknown-alias`). Shared and temp-mail domains only get random local parts, whose alphabet has no `o`, `i` or `l`, so none of these can be drawn; custom domains are their owner's, who may give these addresses out.

**Tests.** Repository (fakecloud and DynamoDB Local): each counter's window, per-key separation, concurrent reservations never passing the limit, login failures stopping at the limit. Turnstile: the fixtures decode and classify; the emulator replays the recordings; the production client on the emulator with each test secret, a wrong secret and an unreachable endpoint. API (`apps/api/test/signup.test.ts`): the widget config, a missing or rejected token, a spent token, failing closed on a wrong secret, the username checked before the token, each limit and its next window, logins still working while sign-up is paused. Aliases and the inbound pipeline: role addresses refused and never created on the fly. Browser: sign-up through the emulated widget (every existing flow), a failed sign-up followed by one with a fresh widget, and the widget contract above.

**Not verified.** A production widget and secret (real tokens, their single use and expiry, hostname checks) need a Cloudflare account; the Lambda's outbound call to `challenges.cloudflare.com`, the SSM grant and CloudFront's CSP need a deploy. The defaults are guesses to tune with real traffic: an address shared by many people (carrier-grade NAT, a university) shares one budget of 30 challenges an hour and 5 accounts a day, which AWS WAF rate rules (SR-8) can complement.

**Decisions for the owner.** The four default numbers; whether invite codes should gate the private beta as well (not built; the Turnstile check stays either way); whether mail to role addresses on user subdomains should reach an operator mailbox instead of being dropped (RFC 5321 §4.5.1 asks for `postmaster` on every domain that accepts mail; the shared domains' own postmaster is unaffected).

### 20.28 Bounce hardening: one crafted message must not stop forwarding (2026-10-23)

Fixes SR-10 (§20.25) as far as AWS's documentation allows. Before, any `Permanent` bounce put every bounced address on the account-level suppression list and paused the owner's recipient; a sender who got the owner's provider to refuse one message for its content (a 5.7.x policy or spam rejection, which SES may still report as `Permanent`/`General`) cut the owner off. On top of that, the configuration set had SES suppress hard-bounced addresses by itself (`suppressedReasons: ["BOUNCE"]`), so even a fixed handler couldn't have kept a content rejection off the list.

**Only what SES documents decides** (event-publishing-retrieving-sns-contents.html: bounce types and subtypes, `bouncedRecipients[].status` and `diagnosticCode`, `mail.messageId`, `mail.destination`, `mail.tags`; RFC 3463 enhanced status codes; RFC 7505 null MX). The documented `Permanent` subtypes are `General`, `NoEmail`, `Suppressed`, `OnAccountSuppressionList`, `EmailValidationSuppressed` and `OnTenantSuppressionList`; content, attachment and size rejections are documented as `Transient` (`ContentRejected`, `AttachmentRejected`, `MessageTooLarge`). There is no `UnsupportedContent` subtype in the docs: an unknown subtype is treated as about the message.

**1. Correlation.** Every send through SES is recorded right after SES accepts it: a `Sent` item in the user's partition (`USER#<userId>` / `SENT#<sesMessageId>`, `SentRepo`, so erasing the account removes it) with the kind (`forward`, `reply`, `confirmation`) and, for forwards and confirmations, the recipient's id, never an address or content, expiring after a week (SES publishes once it stops retrying; a downstream MTA may report days after accepting, RFC 5321 §4.5.4.1 suggests 4–5 days). The feedback Lambda acts on a bounce only if its `mail.messageId` has a live record under the user its `alias-user` tag names, the bounced address is in `mail.destination`, and (for forwards and confirmations) the user's recipient with that id still has that address. Anything else is `Ignored`/`uncorrelated`. Writing the record can fail without failing the send (the mail is out; a retry would send it twice): its bounces are then ignored, which fails safe for forwarding. Four call sites write it: the pipeline's forwards and replies, the API's confirmation mail and outbox sends.

**2. Decision table** (`apps/inbound/src/Bounces.ts`, unit-tested row by row in `apps/inbound/test/bounces.test.ts`). Codes are read from `status` and from every enhanced code in `diagnosticCode`:

| SES type / subtype                                     | Enhanced codes                                                   | Verdict          | Effect for a forward/confirmation recipient                                                                 | For a reply (contact) |
| ------------------------------------------------------ | ---------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------- |
| `Transient` / any, `Undetermined`                      | any                                                              | `MessageOnly`    | none                                                                                                        | none                  |
| `Permanent` / `Suppressed`, `OnAccountSuppressionList` | any                                                              | `Suppressed`     | pause now (`suppressed`); nothing added to the list                                                         | none                  |
| `Permanent` / `General`, `NoEmail`                     | any of 5.2.2, 5.2.3, 5.3.x, 5.6.x, 5.7.x                         | `MessageOnly`    | none, whatever `status` says                                                                                | none                  |
| `Permanent` / `General`, `NoEmail`                     | only 5.1.1 and/or 5.1.10                                         | `AddressUnknown` | suppress + pause now (`address-unknown`)                                                                    | suppress              |
| `Permanent` / `General`, `NoEmail`                     | anything else (5.1.x, 5.2.1, 5.4.x, 5.5.x, 5.0.0, 4.x.x) or none | `AddressSuspect` | count once per message; the **3rd distinct message within 7 days** suppresses + pauses (`repeated-bounces`) | none                  |
| `Permanent` / any other subtype                        | any                                                              | `MessageOnly`    | none                                                                                                        | none                  |

A content or policy code anywhere wins over an address code in `status`: the next message can still prove an address dead, but nothing takes a wrong pause back by itself. The count is per message (`HardBounce` items, `BOUNCE#<recipientId>#<sesMessageId>`, so a redelivered bounce counts once and a re-added address starts afresh) and starts again from the recipient's last confirmation mail or confirmation; the items expire after the window. **Decision for the owner:** threshold 3 in 7 days (SR-10 proposed 2); it costs at most two more bounces per dead mailbox against the shared bounce rate, and an attacker would need three hard rejections that name no content or policy code.

**3. Suppression and resuming.** The configuration set now overrides the account's suppression settings with no reasons (`suppressedReasons: []`, "Override account level settings" without reasons in the SES console), so SES adds nothing by itself; the feedback Lambda puts an address on the account-level list only for `AddressUnknown` and after the threshold. A paused recipient carries `bounceReason` (API: `Recipient.bounceReason`); the web app shows "Paused" with the reason on the destinations screen and the overview, and a **Resume** button for verified ones, which is the existing resend: it takes the address off the suppression list and mails a new link, and confirming it clears the pause (pending recipients still resend as before). Forwarding stays paused until the link is opened, so a dead mailbox costs one more bounce, not a stream of them.

**4. Verified, and what must be re-checked.** fakecloud v0.51.0's simulator bounce (`bounce@simulator.amazonses.com`) is `Permanent`/`General` with `status` 5.1.1 and `diagnosticCode` `smtp; 550 5.1.1 user unknown`, as in AWS's documented sample, and the handler tests and the stack test use it through the real SES → SNS → subscriber chain. fakecloud can't produce other bounces (`suppressionlist@simulator` also comes out as `General` 5.1.1), so content, unclear and suppression-list bounces are published to the same SNS topic in the documented record's shape for a message really sent (`bounceBack` in `@alias/e2e/stack`). Tests: decision table (unit), `SentRepo` and `RecipientRepo.countBounce`/resume on fakecloud and DynamoDB Local, feedback handler on fakecloud (content rejections ignored, three unclear bounces pause, suppression-list subtypes pause without suppressing, replies suppress only for a gone mailbox, uncorrelated bounces ignored), pipeline (sends are recorded), API (resume through resend + confirm), stack (a crafted 5.7.1 content bounce of a real forward leaves forwarding on; a 5.1.1 bounce of the next forward pauses it) and browser (the reason and the Resume button). **Needs a sandbox account:** record bounces for the slots in `packages/aws/fixtures/README.md` (user unknown at Gmail/Outlook/a Postfix host, null MX, a 5.7.x content and a DMARC rejection, a full mailbox, the account and global suppression lists) and check each against the table; confirm that SES's `status` is the DSN's and how often `diagnosticCode` is missing; confirm that the empty-reasons override stops automatic suppression and whether sends through the set still consult the account-level list (the pipeline drops paused recipients itself either way, so forwarding doesn't depend on it).

### 20.29 Capacity and isolation (2026-10-23)

Fixes SR-12, SR-20 and SR-19 from §20.25. The goal: no flood on a public URL (the API, the image proxy) and no single user can take the capacity SES-invoked mail processing needs, and nothing SES hands us is lost silently.

#### Image proxy budgets (SR-12)

Before, anyone could get proxy URLs signed by mailing their own alias with `proxy-images` on, and ten reserved slots × a 5 s deadline was about two uncached fetches a second for everyone.

**Signed URLs carry their owner.** `/img/<key version>/<tag>/<owner>/<mac>/<base64url(URL)>` (`ImageProxy` in `@alias/core`):

- `<tag>`: 72 bits of HMAC over the alias id, so the alias isn't named;
- `<owner>`: the alias owner's user id XORed with an HMAC-derived pad for that tag; the proxy recovers it;
- `<mac>`: HMAC-SHA-256 over tag, owner and URL, 128 bits, as before. Every HMAC has its own domain separator under the same keyring key.

A URL's owner can't be swapped (the MAC covers it), so whoever holds a URL spends only the budget of the user whose mail it came from. Because the pad differs per alias, URLs from two aliases of one owner don't show they belong together. Proxy URLs end up wherever the owner's mail goes (quoted in a reply through the reverse alias, forwarded by hand), and a plain user id there would link aliases the owner keeps apart. Within an alias the same image keeps its URL, so CloudFront caches it once per alias.

**Decision: no signing per reader or per message.** An alias's recipient is its owner, so signing per owner is already per recipient. Binding a URL to a reader or a message would add nothing against sharing: a shared URL spends the sharer's budget. It would also cost the cache. What it does cost: CloudFront keys on the whole path, so a newsletter's image is fetched once per alias that received it, not once for all (§20.19's "fetched once" now means once per alias).

**What a request does now** (`Proxy.ts`, `ProxyRepo` in `@alias/db`), after the signature verified:

| Step                                                                                | Answer when it stops                                                     |
| ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| The origin (`URL.origin`) is marked down                                            | 502, `Retry-After` and `max-age` = the time left (≤ 60 s), nothing spent |
| 60 fetches per alias owner and UTC minute (`ProxyOwnerQuota`)                       | 429, `Retry-After` until the minute ends                                 |
| 300 fetches per origin host and UTC minute, all owners (`ProxyHostQuota`)           | 429, the same                                                            |
| Fetch: connect, first byte and any pause 2 s each (socket idle timeout), 5 s in all | 502 for 5 minutes, as before                                             |
| The table can't be reached                                                          | 503 for 5 s: no fetch without a budget                                   |

The counters are `Quota.reserve` (one conditional `UpdateItem`, atomic under concurrency) on a new minute window (`Quota.window(now, "minute")`). The numbers are `Proxy.defaultBudgets`. A newsletter opened is a few dozen images, so 60 a minute only stops floods.

**Negative caching.** An origin that couldn't be reached at all is marked down for a minute (`ProxyHostDown`, `until` + TTL): no address, connection refused, unreachable, a TLS failure, or no connection within 2 s. Slow answers (no first byte, a stalled body) don't mark it. Their cause can be the path, which the requester picks: one slow resize URL on a big CDN must not blank that CDN's images for everyone. That URL's own 502 is cached for 5 minutes by CloudFront, as before. Marks are keyed by origin (scheme, host, port), so a refused `http://cdn` leaves `https://cdn` alone.

**Capacity, worked out.** The proxy has 20 reserved slots. One owner's 60 fetches a minute hold at most 2 s each against a silent origin (2 slots on average), 5 s against one that trickles bytes (5 slots). Unreachable origins cost one connect timeout a minute. Before, one requester could hold every slot. Saturating the proxy now takes several accounts. Free sign-ups (SR-9) and per-user WAF rules on the distribution (SR-8; the owner is a path segment, so a rule can match it) are what cap that. Images degrade, mail is unaffected.

**With CloudFront (§20.26), by headers only.** The proxy sends `Cache-Control: public, max-age=…` on every answer and `Retry-After` on 429/502/503. CloudFront caches 502 and 503 for their `max-age` (it caches only 400, 403, 404, 405, 414, 416 and 5xx errors), and doesn't cache 429. A refused URL asked again reaches the Lambda, which refuses it with one failing conditional write and no fetch. The cache key stays the path (CachingOptimized).

**Isolation.** The proxy's role gets `GetItem`, `PutItem` and `UpdateItem` on the table only for items whose partition key starts with `PROXY#` (`dynamodb:LeadingKeys`), so it still reads nothing of anyone's aliases, contacts or tokens. The counters hold a user id for a minute plus TTL lag. Erasure doesn't sweep them; they expire on their own.

**Cost.** One eventually consistent `GetItem` and two `UpdateItem`s per uncached fetch: about $1.5 per million at on-demand prices, next to Lambda's ~$0.9 (§20.19). Not checked against a bill.

#### Reserved concurrency and lost events (SR-20)

Every function now has reserved concurrency (`infra/alchemy.run.ts`, `reservedConcurrency`). Each number both guarantees that many concurrent executions and caps the function there. Reserving costs nothing; only use is billed.

| Function         | Reserved | Why                                                                                      |
| ---------------- | -------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| inbound          | 100      | SES mail at ~1 s each: bursts of ~100 messages a second                                  |
| feedback         | 10       | one SNS notification per bounce or complaint                                             |
| webhook delivery | 20       | the event source mapping's `MaximumConcurrency` is the same, so batches aren't throttled |
| image proxy      | 20       | the budgets above share it (was 10)                                                      |
| API              | 200      | a cap more than a reservation: a flood on the public URL stops there                     |
| verify cron      | 2        | one run every ten minutes                                                                |

That's 352 in all. Lambda keeps at least 100 unreserved, so the account's concurrency quota must be **at least 452**. The default is 1,000, but new accounts may start with as few as 10, and then the deploy fails. **The stack asks for 1,000** (Service Quotas → AWS Lambda → "Concurrent executions", `L-B99A9384`, eu-west-1, since §20.37; until AWS grants it, the reserved functions fail to deploy). At list prices concurrency itself is free; the cost model (§14.1) is unchanged.

**Async events are recorded, not dropped.** SES invokes the inbound Lambda and SNS invokes the feedback Lambda asynchronously. Both now have `maximumRetryAttempts: 2`, `maximumEventAge: 6 hours` (the maximum), and an on-failure destination, the SQS queue `alias-async-failures-<stage>` (14 days, SSE; each role may `SendMessage` to it). A throttled event waits in Lambda's queue for up to 6 hours, then lands there with its original event (`requestPayload`), instead of vanishing. The spool keeps mail 3 days (was 1) so a redrive still finds it.

**Redrive runbook** (needs AWS; no code reads the record, so no schema or emulator is needed yet):

```sh
q=$(aws sqs get-queue-url --queue-name alias-async-failures-<stage> --query QueueUrl --output text)
aws sqs receive-message --queue-url "$q" --max-number-of-messages 1 > m.json
jq -r '.Messages[0].Body | fromjson | .requestPayload' m.json > event.json
aws lambda invoke --function-name <inbound or feedback> --invocation-type Event \
  --cli-binary-format raw-in-base64-out --payload file://event.json /dev/null
aws sqs delete-message --queue-url "$q" --receipt-handle "$(jq -r '.Messages[0].ReceiptHandle' m.json)"
```

Redriving is safe: delivery claims (§20.6) drop recipients already handled, and a message whose spool object is gone is dropped as `spool-missing`.

**Alarms** on the §20.13 alert topic. Its policy now admits every alarm named `alias-<stage>-*`, and the SES alarms keep their names. Each alarm uses one 5-minute period, and missing data is treated as fine:

| Alarm                   | Metric                                                         | Fires at |
| ----------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------- | -------- |
| `inbound-throttled`     | `AWS/Lambda` `Throttles`, inbound (Sum)                        | ≥ 1      |
| `inbound-backlog`       | `AWS/Lambda` `AsyncEventAge`, inbound (Maximum)                | ≥ 10 min |
| `feedback-throttled`    | `AWS/Lambda` `Throttles`, feedback (Sum)                       | ≥ 1      |
| `async-failures`        | `AWS/SQS` `ApproximateNumberOfMessagesVisible`, failures queue | ≥ 1      |
| `api-throttled`         | `Throttles`, API (Sum)                                         | ≥ 100    |
| `image-proxy-throttled` | `Throttles`, image proxy (Sum)                                 | ≥ 100    |

Runbook: inbound or feedback throttled, or a backlog, means raise its reservation (and the quota) or find what keeps the function slow. A failure in the queue means redrive it (above). A throttled API or proxy is the cap working: look for the flood's source (CloudFront logs, WAF with SR-8).

#### Contacts after the cap (SR-19)

A new sender's contact was written while planning, before the hourly cap (`reserveForward`) ran in `admit`, so a rate-limited flood with changing `From` addresses kept writing contact items. Now planning only reads the contact; a known blocked one is still refused without spending the cap. `admit` checks loops, then the cap, then creates the contact and mints the reverse token. `Pipeline.Send.admit` returns what to send (`Outgoing`: contact id, rewrite, envelope) or the refusal.

#### Tests

- **Core:** `ImageProxy`: owner round trip, no user or alias id in the URL, two aliases unlinkable, the same URL within an alias, swapped owner or tag refused, retired keys, malformed paths. The golden corpus case `forward-mailchimp-proxied` was regenerated and read back with Python's `email` package.
- **Repository, both engines:** `ProxyRepo`: per-owner and per-host caps under concurrency, the next minute, down marks expiring and moving on.
- **Egress:** a silent origin is `no first byte`, a stalled one `stalled`, neither marks the origin; a refused connection names its origin.
- **Handler on fakecloud and a local origin:** the owner's budget (429 with `Retry-After: 60` on a stopped clock, another owner unaffected), the host's budget shared by owners, an unreachable origin left alone for any path while another port answers, a slow answer cut after the idle deadline without blaming its host, production configuration with the table.
- **Pipeline on fakecloud:** a rate-limited flood from four addresses leaves one contact (fails on `main`: three), and proxied URLs verify to the alias owner.
- **Stack:** the image proxy in `e2e` runs with its budgets on the shared table.
- **Infra:** typechecked only.

**Needs a real AWS account.** The concurrency quota increase and the reservations applying; `dynamodb:LeadingKeys` with `ForAllValues:StringLike` on `UpdateItem`, `PutItem` and `GetItem`; the on-failure record's shape (`requestPayload`) and that throttled events reach it after `maximumEventAge`; the `AsyncEventAge` and `Throttles` metrics and the alarms; CloudFront not caching 429 and honouring `max-age` on 502/503; how Lambda's network reports connect failures (the error codes `Egress` treats as unreachable); real fetch latencies, to tune 2 s and the budgets.

### 20.30 Remaining hardening from the security review (2026-10-23)

The open findings of §20.25 that need no AWS account and no owner decision, one PR each, stacked: SR-18, SR-15 + SR-17, SR-14, SR-13, and the cheap parts of SR-25/SR-26 (marked **Partly fixed**). Each fix comes with tests that fail without it. The finding list in §20.25 marks them **Fixed (§20.30)**.

#### SR-18: inline styles through a CSS parser

Inline styles are kept (mail layout lives in them: dropping every `style` would collapse most HTML mail into one column of unstyled text), but no longer filtered by matching text. sanitize-html already parses each `style` attribute with PostCSS; `allowedStyles` now checks the parsed declarations: an allowlist of about 50 formatting properties under their exact names (an escaped name such as `b\61ckground` is another name), and a value of letters, digits, units, `#`, quotes, commas and slashes only. Every way CSS loads something is a function (`url()`, `image-set()`, `image()`, `cross-fade()`, `element()`, `var()` holding one, `expression()`), and every way to disguise one needs a backslash or a comment, so no parenthesis, backslash, `@` or `*` gets through; the one exception is a whole value that is `rgb()`/`rgba()`/`hsl()`/`hsla()` of numbers on `color`, `background-color` and `border-color`. `position`, `z-index` and the like are not on the list, so a message can't lay a fake dialog over the reader's screen. What is lost: `calc()`, gradients, `border: 1px solid rgb(…)` (the hex form stays), and CSS variables. The HTML that the API, SDK, CLI and MCP return as "sanitized" now holds without the web app's CSP. Tests (`packages/inbox/test/sanitize.test.ts`): escaped `url()` with and without a space, entity-encoded, upper case, `image-set()`, `-webkit-image-set()`, `image()`, `cross-fade()`, a comment inside, an escaped property, a custom property through `var()`, `list-style-image`, `border-image`, `content`, `@import`, `expression()`, a `}` breaking out of the declaration block; ordinary formatting (colors, fonts, borders, `!important`) kept; `position: fixed` dropped. The old filter let six of them through.

#### SR-15: the consent screen vouches only for what is verified

A client's `client_name` is its own claim whatever its kind, so the consent card no longer leads with it. `POST /v1/oauth/authorization` now returns `client.host`, the host of a metadata-document client's `client_id` URL (the host that served the document, the one name of who asks that is verified; none for registered clients), and `client.uri` only when the `client_uri` is on that host or below it with the same scheme and no credentials, so never for registered clients. The card is titled `Connect <host>?` for metadata documents and "Connect this app?" for registered clients, lists the name as "Calls itself “…”" with a "Not verified" badge for both kinds, shows the website as a link (it is same-site now), and keeps the redirect host in the description. "Return to the app" on an error that goes back to the client is now "Return to `<host>`", naming where the client's chosen URI leads. Tests: API (`oauth.test.ts`: a registered client's `client_uri` and `host` are absent; a document's `host`; its `client_uri` kept on its own origin and dropped for another host, a lookalike `127.0.0.1.evil.example`, the host in a path, another scheme, userinfo and a non-URL), browser (`browser/test/oauth.test.ts`: the card of a registered "Claude" with `client_uri: https://claude.ai` says "Connect this app?", "“Claude”", "Not verified", has no link and no `claude.ai`; a refusal's button reads "Return to 127.0.0.1:5555").

#### SR-17: unused registered clients expire; IPv6 counts by /64

A client registered through RFC 7591 now carries `expiresAt` 30 days after registration (the table's TTL attribute; `Table.ts` lists it), and readers treat it as gone from then on, since TTL deletes late (§20.3). The transaction that redeems a registered client's first code also `REMOVE`s its `expiresAt`, conditioned on the client still existing and not having expired, so a client in use stays for good and a code whose client lapsed meanwhile fails like a used one (`invalid_grant`); metadata-document clients aren't stored and are left alone. Every per-address limit (registrations, token and MCP failures, device requests) now counts an IPv6 address by its /64 (`ClientAddress.rateLimitKey` in core; IPv4-mapped addresses count as their IPv4 address): a /64 is the smallest block one subscriber gets (RFC 6177), so one connection can no longer rotate through 2^64 addresses. A /64 shared by many users would share their allowance; behind CloudFront that will also hold for `CloudFront-Viewer-Address` (SR-8). The IPv6 parser moved from `WebhookUrl` to `internal/ip.ts`, shared by both. Tests: core (`ClientAddress.test.ts`), repository on both engines (`oauthRepoSuite.ts`: an unused client is gone at its expiry while a used one stays; a code whose registered client expired or vanished is `CodeUnusable` and leaves no grant), API (`oauth.test.ts`: a registered client still known after 29 days, unknown after 30, unless a grant used it; ten registrations from one /64 exhaust it for the whole /64 but not the next).

#### SR-14: cookie-authenticated changes must come from the app

`Authentication.session` now refuses, with `CrossSiteRequest` (403, in the contract and the OpenAPI document), any request other than `GET`/`HEAD`/`OPTIONS` whose `Sec-Fetch-Site` isn't `same-origin` or whose `Origin` isn't the relying party's origin (`ALIAS_WEB_ORIGIN`), or that carries neither header; and one with a body whose `Content-Type` isn't `application/json` (any case, any parameters). Every current browser sends `Origin` on non-GET requests and `Sec-Fetch-Site` on every fetch, and no page can set either, so the web app passes and another page can't: not with a `no-cors` blob (no content type, which HttpApi would have read as JSON), not from a sibling subdomain (`same-site` is not `same-origin`), not from a sandboxed frame (`Origin: null`, which is what the inbox's HTML frame would send). The body check reads the request text, which the request caches, so the payload decoder reads the same text afterwards. Bearer-token requests are untouched: the token scheme runs only when the session scheme fails, and a valid token wins whatever the headers say. Because HttpApi answers with the _last_ scheme's failure, the token scheme, finding no token on a request that carries a session cookie, repeats the check so the client hears `CrossSiteRequest` rather than 401. Not covered: `POST /v1/auth/logout`, which needs no session and only ends one (left to `SameSite=Strict`), and the sign-up/login ceremonies, which a cross-site page can't complete without a passkey assertion for our RP ID; the OAuth token and registration endpoints use no cookie. This stands on its own without SR-8; once CloudFront serves app and API from one origin, `ALIAS_WEB_ORIGIN` stays that origin. Tests (`apps/api/test/csrf.test.ts`): another site's `no-cors` blob POST, a same-site subdomain, `Origin: null`, a same-origin body sent as no type, `text/plain` or a form, a body-less `DELETE` from elsewhere, a change with neither header are all 403 `CrossSiteRequest` and create nothing; either header alone saying the app, with `Application/JSON; charset=utf-8`, passes; a bearer token passes with no browser headers and with cross-site ones. The browser suite (real Chromium) exercises the app's own requests through the check.

#### SR-13: webhook signing secrets sealed at rest

Signing needs the secret itself, so it can't be a hash like every other credential in the table; it is now sealed, as §20.25 proposed, with a fourth SSM keyring, `/alias/<stage>/webhook-keys` (`ALIAS_WEBHOOK_KEYS_PARAMETER`). No new AWS service, so no new emulator: Parameter Store is already verified (§20.14).

- **Shared code.** The AES-256-GCM primitives moved out of `@alias/inbox` `Envelope` into core's `Seal` (`importKey`, `deriveKey` with HKDF-SHA-256 under a per-use label, `seal`/`open` with AAD, `SealError`, which replaces `EnvelopeError`). `Envelope` keeps its format byte for byte; a known-answer test opens a message the previous code sealed.
- **`Secrets` in `@alias/webhooks`.** `seal(owner, secret)` uses the keyring's current key under the label `alias/webhook/kek/v1`, AAD `alias/webhook-secret/v1 ‖ userId ‖ webhookId`; the stored blob is `key version (1 byte) ‖ Seal blob`. `open` picks that keyring entry. The binding names no slot, so a rotation moves the sealed bytes from `sealedSecret` to `previousSealedSecret` unchanged, atomically in one `UpdateItem` as before; a secret copied onto another webhook's item doesn't open.
- **Who holds the keys.** The API seals on create and rotate (the plaintext only goes into the one response that shows it); the delivery Lambda opens. Producers (API, inbound, feedback) only enqueue job ids and get no access. Both read the parameter at cold start; the delivery Lambda's entrypoint now builds its services with top-level await like the API's, so a transient SSM error fails the init instead of poisoning the container (§20.14). A secret that doesn't open (its version left the keyring, or the item was tampered with) is retried, not signed with, so the job ends in the dead-letter queue intact.
- **Infra.** Env var and `ssm:GetParameter` on that one ARN for the API and the delivery Lambda; the parameter is put by hand before a stage's first deploy (command in `infra/alchemy.run.ts`). Rotation is the usual two puts; a retired version must stay until every secret it sealed has been rotated or its webhook deleted (re-sealing in bulk would need a one-off script: not written, nothing is deployed). The 2³² seals-per-key bound of random IVs (SR-27) is far off: one seal per create or rotate.
- **Migration.** `WebhookRepo` stores `sealedSecret`/`previousSealedSecret` (binary) instead of `secret`/`previousSecret` (string). Nothing is deployed, so this is a clean switch with no migration code: old items in a dev table (`.dev/fakecloud`) no longer decode, and listing that user's webhooks fails until they are deleted (or the dev data reset). `pnpm dev` puts fixed dev keys as `/alias/dev/webhook-keys` (API and delivery read the same ones).
- **Tests.** Repository on both engines (sealed bytes stored and rotated as given); `@alias/webhooks` delivery (signing with the current and the rotated-out secret after opening both; a secret moved to another webhook is retried and nothing is sent); the delivery Lambda built from its environment with keys from fakecloud's SSM; API (`webhooks.test.ts`: the table scan holds none of three secrets' key material; the stored current and previous secrets open to the ones shown; opening under another webhook fails); `@alias/inbox` (`Envelope` on `Seal`, known answer); stack (`e2e` reads the webhook keys from fakecloud's SSM for both Lambdas and verifies delivered signatures).

#### SR-25/SR-26: sessions and passkeys in Settings, the counter only goes up

The cheap parts. A new session-only `SecurityGroup` (`packages/contracts/src/Security.ts`, a token gets 403): `GET /v1/sessions` lists the account's live sessions (`id` is the SHA-256 the table keys them by, never the cookie; `isCurrent` marks the caller's), `DELETE /v1/sessions/:id` ends one of the caller's own (`SessionNotFound` for anyone else's, conditioned on `userId` in the `DeleteItem`), `DELETE /v1/sessions` ends every other one and says how many, and `GET /v1/passkeys` lists the passkeys with when they were added, last used and their transports. Both lists read the existing gsi1 partitions (`SESSIONS#`/`PASSKEYS#<userId>`), so no table change; gsi1 is eventually consistent on AWS, so a session created a moment ago may be missing from the list and from "everywhere else" until the index catches up (usually under a second; not verifiable on the emulators, which answer at once). Settings replaces its static "Passkey" card with the passkey list and adds a Sessions section (design-system cards, "This browser" badge, "Sign out", "Sign out everywhere else"). Sessions carry no device label: the session item stores none, and adding the user agent was left out to keep this cheap.

`PasskeyRepo.recordUse` now also stores `lastUsedAt`, and writes the counter only if it goes up (`#counter < :counter`) or the authenticator doesn't count (`:counter = 0`); a refused write is read back to tell a vanished passkey (`PasskeyNotFound`) from a counter that didn't increase (`CounterNotIncreased`, answered as `PasskeyRejected: verification-failed`). SimpleWebAuthn already compares the counter with the stored one, so this only closes the race between two concurrent assertions with the same stored value (one could have written a lower counter last, so a cloned authenticator's later use would pass).

Not done (not cheap, or a decision): a fresh passkey assertion before `POST /v1/outbox/:id/send` and device approval (UI flows in the web app and CLI), an idle timeout, deleting or adding passkeys (a second passkey needs a registration ceremony for a signed-in user; deleting the last one locks the account out), counting device-code misses per user and code, and letting a domain's DNS proof take over a pending claim.

Tests: repository on both engines (sessions listed oldest first without expired or others' ones, `deleteOwned` refuses another user's; passkeys listed per user; the counter refuses equal and lower values, keeps the higher one, and lets 0 through every time, `lastUsedAt` stored), API (`security.test.ts`: three browsers of one account, one session ended, then the others at once with the caller's kept; another user's session id is 404 and untouched; the passkey list shows `lastUsedAt` after a login; tokens get 403), browser (`browser/test/settings.test.ts`: the same passkey on a second browser, as a synced passkey would be, signs in there; Settings on the first lists the passkey and both sessions, "Sign out everywhere else" leaves one, and the second browser's session is gone).

### 20.31 Organising aliases: recipients per alias, descriptions, labels, export and import (2026-10-23)

addy.io parity for people with many aliases. Everything is per alias and lives on the alias item or in the owner's partition, so erasure, deletion and the forward path needed no new access pattern.

**Recipients per alias.** An alias may name up to five of its owner's verified recipients (`recipientIds`, a list on the alias item, `MAX_ALIAS_RECIPIENTS`). `Policy.forwardTargets` picks the named ones that still exist and are verified, in the alias's order; when it names none of those (none chosen, or all deleted or pending since) mail goes to the default recipient of the username whose subdomain the alias is on (§20.34) while that one is verified, else to the account's default recipient. So an alias's own choice overrides its username's, which overrides the account's. Paused (bounced) recipients get nothing and the others still do; when every target is paused the mail is dropped as `recipient-bounced`, not redirected to the default, because the owner chose those mailboxes. **No extra read**: the forward path already lists the owner's recipients with one Query (for loop detection), and the ids come with the alias item. The API accepts only the caller's own verified recipients (`InvalidAliasRecipient`: `unknown` or `unverified`, 400), unique ids, at most five; a recipient deleted later just disappears from the alias's view and export. Replies are accepted from any of the owner's verified recipients, as before (§20.6), so every mailbox an alias forwards to can reply.

**One copy per mailbox.** A forward to several recipients sends one SES message per recipient (`Outgoing.copies` in `Pipeline.ts`): the header rewrite and scrubbing run once, then each copy is sealed for its mailbox (PGP/MIME to that recipient's key, §20.21, or as it is), sent, and remembered under its own SES message ID with its `recipientId` (§20.28), so bounces stay per mailbox. Idempotency (§20.6) keeps its shape: the message's claim per alias address comes first, and with more than one copy each copy is claimed too (`DELIVERY#<sesMessageId>` / `RECIPIENT#<address>#TO#<recipientId>`, one conditional PutItem each, a TargetDelivery in `Table.ts`). A copy SES can't take now releases its own claim and defers the record; Lambda's retry finds the copies already sent claimed and sends only the missing ones, and the message is counted (and `mail.forwarded` published) once, by the invocation that finishes it. A copy SES refuses (`rejected`) or whose key expired (`key-unusable`) is dropped alone and logged; when every copy is refused, the message is refused as a single-recipient one would be. A single recipient costs exactly what it did (no per-copy claim). The hourly cap counts the message once, whatever the number of copies.

**Descriptions and labels.** `description` (trimmed, at most 200 characters, empty removes it) and `labels` (at most 10, unique; each 1–32 Unicode letters, digits, spaces, `.`, `_` or `-`, starting with a letter or digit, so a list of labels is one unambiguous CSV cell) are optional on `POST /v1/aliases` and `PATCH /v1/aliases/:domain/:local`, and every `AliasView` now also carries them, `recipientIds` and `createdAt`. Empty lists aren't stored. A label may have a colour: `GET /v1/labels` lists the caller's labels (from their aliases, plus coloured ones not in use yet) with colour and alias count, `PUT /v1/labels/:label { color }` sets or (`null`) clears it (`aliases:read`/`aliases:write`). Colours are names from a fixed palette (`LabelColor`: gray, red, orange, yellow, green, teal, blue, violet, pink), never CSS, so nothing a user types reaches a style; one `LabelColor` item per coloured label in the user's partition (`LABEL#<label>`, at most 50, a soft cap like the webhooks').

**Export.** `GET /v1/aliases/export` returns `{ exportedAt, aliases }`, each with address, active, mode, description, labels, recipients **by address** (empty: the default recipient), counters and `createdAt`; temporary inboxes are left out like in the list. Because it names recipients' addresses, a token needs `aliases:read` **and** `recipients:read`: `requires(...)` now takes several scopes, all required, and the OpenAPI document lists them all; the alias list itself names recipients by id only, so `aliases:read` keeps not revealing the owner's mailboxes. CSV is made by the clients from that JSON with `AliasCsv.format` (`@alias/core`, shared by the web app and the CLI): RFC 4180 quoting, CRLF, and every cell starting with `=`, `+`, `-`, `@`, a tab or a carriage return gets a leading `'` (OWASP's advice on CSV injection; local parts created on the fly are chosen by senders, so this isn't only about the owner's own text). Checked independently by reading the output back with Python's `csv` module.

**Import.** `POST /v1/aliases/import { aliases: [{ address, description?, labels?, isActive? }] }` (1–100 rows, `aliases:write`) creates each address the way `POST /v1/aliases` with an `Address` does, so only the caller's subdomain and verified custom domains (shared domains stay random-only), and answers one result per row: `Created` with the alias, or `Failed` with the reason (`InvalidAlias`'s reasons, `taken`, `duplicate` within the request, `invalid-description`, `invalid-labels`). One bad row fails only itself. `AliasCsv.parse` reads a file by its header (any column order and letter case; `address` required; `description`, `labels` separated by `;`, `active` as true/false, yes/no, 1/0; other columns ignored; a byte order mark, LF or CRLF, quoted line breaks), and strips the `'` the export added, so an export imports again. Recipients aren't imported (ids differ between accounts); the web app and CLI send files in chunks of 100. Creating aliases through the API has no daily cap (the on-the-fly cap of §20.8 is about mail creating them); an import is a hundred API calls' worth per request.

**Web app** (Aliases screen, design system of §20.16). Each row shows its description under the address and its labels as badges, coloured by a dot from a fixed class map (Tailwind's own zinc/red/orange/yellow/emerald/teal/sky/violet/pink, not new design tokens: a deliberate small exception to §20.16's "colour only for status", since labels are the user's own categories). The search box also matches descriptions and labels; a "Filter by label" select (`?label=`) joins the domain and status filters. "Edit" in the row menu opens the "Edit alias" dialog (`?edit=<address>`): description, labels as comma-separated text (checked against the same rules before saving), and one "Forward to <address>" switch per verified destination (at most five; none chosen shows the default destination); only changed fields are sent. "Labels" (`?dialog=labels`) colours each label; "Export CSV" downloads `aliases.csv`; "Import" (`?dialog=import`) reads a CSV file, sends it in chunks of 100 and lists a result per file row.

**Surfaces.** CLI: `alias aliases edit <alias> [--description] [--label …|--no-labels] [--recipient <address> …|--default-recipient]` (recipients by address, resolved through `recipients:read`, which the CLI already asks for), `alias aliases export` (CSV to stdout, `--json` for the API's JSON), `alias aliases import <file>` (a result per row), and the list shows labels. MCP: `list_aliases` shows descriptions and labels, and `set_alias_details` sets them (`aliases:write`); recipients stay out of MCP on purpose, since choosing where mail goes is not something an assistant should change on its own. All three surfaces call the same handlers (`apps/api/src/aliases.ts`); nothing is duplicated.

**Erasure and deletion** (§20.15). Deleting an alias removes its description, labels and recipient ids with the rest of the item (the tombstone still holds only `deletedAt` and `userId`, tested); erasing the account deletes the LabelColor items with the rest of the user's partition (the `AccountRepo` suite now seeds aliases with all three and a label colour, and still finds only the four tombstones).

**Tests.** Core (`AliasCsv`: exact output, quoting, every formula prefix, round trip, header order and case, BOM, blank lines, `active` spellings, refusals), repository on fakecloud and DynamoDB Local (details stored, changed, removed alone and next to other changes, label colours per user), unit (`Policy.forwardTargets`: order, gone and pending ids skipped, fallback), API handler (`apps/api/test/organisation.test.ts`: create and change with details, verified-only recipients and every refused payload, a deleted recipient disappears, export contents and the two-scope rule for tokens, import results row by row, labels with colours and the cap; MCP `set_alias_details`), pipeline on fakecloud (one copy per recipient in order with its own trace, fallback, a paused recipient skipped and all paused dropped, one copy refused by SES, PGP to one copy only, one key expired, a copy sent by an earlier invocation not sent again after a deferral, counted once), CLI against the API (edit, CSV and JSON export, import of the export plus new rows, a file without an address column), stack (`e2e`: an alias forwarding to two confirmed mailboxes over SMTP, a reply from the second, the export naming both) and browser (`browser/test/organisation.test.ts`: describe and label aliases with an invalid label refused first, filter by label, search by description, colour a label, export and check the downloaded CSV incl. the `'` before a description starting with `=`; choose two verified destinations in order and go back to the default; import a file with one good and one shared-domain row). Not verified: the download in browsers other than headless Chromium, and an import above 100 rows (chunking) in a test.

### 20.32 Global blocklist and rules (2026-10-23)

addy.io has rules; SimpleLogin blocks per contact. We now have both on top of contacts (§20.9): a blocklist that acts on every alias of a user, and ordered rules that tag, reroute or block mail.

**Storage: one item per user, read once per message.** The forward path must stay cheap, so a user's blocklist and rules live in one `USER#<userId>` / `FILTERS` item (`FilterRepo`, `Table.spec`, suite on fakecloud and DynamoDB Local). The inbound Lambda reads it with one strongly consistent GetItem per recipient, however many entries it holds. Per-entry items were rejected: a sender would cost a GetItem for the address plus one per label of its domain (`*.a.b.example`, `*.b.example`, …). The blocklist is a string set (`blocked`), so adding and removing are single `ADD`/`DELETE` UpdateItems that never lose a concurrent change, with the cap in the condition (`size(blocked) < :limit OR contains(blocked, :id)`, so re-adding at the cap is harmless). The rules are JSON (`rules`) with a `rulesVersion`: each change is read, changed, and written conditionally on the version it read, retried up to five times. Bounds: 500 entries (≤ 254 bytes each), 30 rules of at most 5 conditions and 5 actions with ≤ 200-character values, so the item stays well under DynamoDB's 400 KB (worst case ~250 KB; a typical user's is far below one 4 KB read unit).

**Blocklist** (`Blocklist` in `@alias/core`, pure). An entry is its own id: `news@shop.example` (that address), `shop.example` (the domain only) or `*.shop.example` (the domain and every subdomain). A sender is checked against the set by its candidates (the address, its domain, `*.` + the domain and each parent), so the lookup is O(labels), and the most specific entry is named. It matches the `From` sender (else the envelope sender), the identity contacts use. Mail from a listed sender is dropped as `blocked-sender` and counted as blocked on whichever alias it was sent to, inbox aliases included; a reply to a listed contact is dropped too, like a blocked contact's.

**Rules** (`Rules` in `@alias/core`, pure, exhaustively unit-tested). Conditions look at the sender address, the sender domain, the subject (encoded-words decoded, the first 2,000 characters) the alias address or the alias's labels (§20.31: met when any of its labels compares true, so a negated one when none does), with `equals`, `contains`, `starts-with` or `ends-with`, optionally negated, compared after NFC normalization and lowercasing. No user regular expressions: a pattern a user wrote could stall the Lambda (ReDoS), and these four comparisons cover what addy.io's rules are used for; each is linear in the input. A rule matches on `all` or `any` of its conditions; disabled rules are skipped. Every matching rule applies, in order; a `block` stops the rules after it (its own other actions, a webhook say, still apply). Later `set-subject`, `set-display-name` and `forward-to` override earlier ones, prefixes stack with the later one in front, and a `set-subject` drops earlier prefixes.

| Action                          | What forwarding does                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                            |
| ------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| `block`                         | Dropped as `rule-blocked`, counted as blocked                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                   |
| `set-subject`, `prefix-subject` | `Mail.forward` replaces every incoming `Subject` with one field: the original decoded (encoded-words, raw UTF-8 per RFC 6532), changed, written as one sanitized line with encoded-words where needed (a rule's text can't inject a header)                                                                                                     |
| `set-display-name`              | `From` and `Reply-To` show that name on the reverse alias instead of `Name (sender@x)`                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                          |
| `skip-tracker-removal`          | Tracker protection and the image proxy are off for this message (§20.19)                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                        |
| `skip-encryption`               | Sent unencrypted to a destination with an OpenPGP key (§20.21)                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                  |
| `forward-to`                    | That destination alone, if it is verified and not bouncing (`Policy.ruleTarget`); it replaces the alias's own recipients (§20.31) and the username's default (§20.34) for this message. Otherwise those apply as usual (`Policy.forwardTargets`), so mail is never lost to a stale rule. The API refuses a recipient id the caller doesn't have |
| `webhook`                       | A `rule.matched` event (`aliasId`, `address`, `ruleId`; metadata only), published once the delivery is claimed and keyed by message, recipient and rule, so a redelivery repeats the `webhook-id`                                                                                                                                               |

**Where it runs.** `Pipeline.plan` evaluates the blocklist and rules after the existing policy checks (inactive alias, expired inbox, virus and spam verdicts) and before any rewrite, with what the SES event already holds (sender, `commonHeaders.subject`). Inbox mode only blocks and announces: stored mail is kept as it came. On the reply path the counterparty is the contact the reverse alias names, and only blocking applies: the blocklist and a rule's `block` drop the reply (not counted), nothing else of a rule touches what the owner sends.

**API** (`BlocklistGroup`, `RulesGroup`; scopes `rules:read` / `rules:write`, which cover both because both name third parties' addresses): `GET/POST /v1/blocklist`, `DELETE /v1/blocklist/:entryId`; `GET/POST /v1/rules`, `PUT/DELETE /v1/rules/:ruleId`, `POST /v1/rules/order { ruleIds }` (a permutation of all of them), and `POST /v1/rules/test { sample, rule? }`, which runs the saved blocklist and rules (or one unsaved rule, `draft`) on a made-up message and says what would happen, storing nothing. OpenAPI regenerated.

**Surfaces.** Web: a **Blocklist** screen (add an address or domain, with "block its subdomains too", list, Unblock) and a **Rules** screen (ordered list with an enabled switch, Move up/down, Edit and Delete; a "New rule" dialog, `?dialog=new` / `?edit=<id>`, built with TanStack Form arrays; and "Try a message", the test endpoint). The contacts sheet gains **Block everywhere**. Accessible names: navigation "Blocklist", "Rules"; textbox "Sender", checkbox "For a domain, block its subdomains too", button "Block sender", "Unblock <entry>", table "Blocked senders"; "New rule", dialog "New rule"/"Edit rule" with textboxes "Name", "Condition n text", "Action n text", comboboxes "Condition n field", "Condition n comparison", "Action n", "Action n destination", buttons "Add condition", "Add action", "Save rule"; list "Rules" with items named by rule, switch "<rule> enabled", "Move <rule> up/down"; tester textboxes "Sender", "Subject", "Alias", "Alias labels" (comma-separated), button "Test", status "Test result".
