MCP: `list_blocklist`, `block_sender` (with `isBlocked: false` to unblock). CLI: `alias blocklist list|add [--subdomains]|remove`, `alias rules list` (rules are written in the web app); `alias login` asks for the two new scopes.

**Erasure** (§20.15). The FILTERS item is in the user's partition, so the sweep's step 4 deletes it; the `AccountRepo` suite seeds a blocklist and a rule and finds nothing left.

**Tests.** Core (`Rules`: every field × match type, met and not, negated, labels any-of and none-of when negated; Unicode forms and case; encoded subjects; missing sender/subject; a 2 MB subject stays under 200 ms; all/any, disabled, order, overrides, stacking prefixes, a blocking rule's webhook; schema bounds and unknown fields, match types and actions refused. `Blocklist`: normalizing, stored forms, exact domain vs subdomains vs lookalikes, most specific entry. `Mail.forward` edits: prefix over a multi-charset subject, every `Subject` replaced, raw UTF-8 read, CR/LF injection impossible, display name, nothing changed without edits), repository on both engines (per-user isolation, one item with a real string set, idempotent add, the cap under 8 concurrent adds, removing the last entry, rules order and the blocklist beside them, refusals write nothing, 4 racing rule writes all land), unit (`forwardTarget` with a rule's recipient), pipeline on fakecloud (a blocked sender on two aliases and its replies, a domain tree on an inbox alias, subject and display name rewritten with a `rule.matched` event once across a redelivery, rule blocks counted and only blocks reach replies, `forward-to` verified vs pending, a rule's destination replacing an alias's own recipients and falling back to them, a label condition blocking one of two aliases, tracker removal skipped), API handler (CRUD, order validation, bounds, no regex, foreign recipients, the 30-rule cap, the tester, scopes for tokens, 401), MCP and CLI against the API, stack (`e2e`: a domain tree blocked and a rule with a non-ASCII prefix and a display name, both over the API, then SMTP through the SES emulator), browser (`rules`: Block everywhere from a contact, the Blocklist screen adding a domain tree and unblocking, a rule written in the dialog with a second action picked from the select, its tag on a forwarded mail, the tester, and the switch turning it off across a reload).

**Not verified / open.** Nothing new from AWS: the string-set `ADD`/`DELETE` with `size()`/`contains()` conditions agree on fakecloud and DynamoDB Local, and the inbound role already has GetItem. Whether SES's `commonHeaders.subject` arrives decoded is undocumented (the rules decode encoded-words either way). Rules see the subject SES parsed, not every `Subject` field of a crafted message; they are a convenience, not a security boundary. No per-alias scoping of a rule except through the `alias` condition, and no regex, by decision.

### 20.33 Send new mail from an alias, and failed deliveries (2026-10-23)

Two addy.io features on the paths that already exist: writing to someone who never wrote to the alias, and seeing (and, opt-in, getting back) mail that could not be forwarded. No new AWS service and no second sending path: composed mail is a draft sent through the outbox's `send` (§20.24), mail from the owner's mailbox is a reply (§20.6), and a resend is a forward's last step.

#### Writing first: compose in the app, or a send-to address

**Compose** (`POST /v1/outbox { from, to, subject, text }`, `OutboxGroup.compose`, session-only). The recipient becomes a contact of the alias (`contacts.ensure`; a known one, blocked or not, is found, a blocked one refuses), then a draft is made and sent at once with the same `outbox.send` a confirmed MCP draft uses: alias and contact checked again, the daily allowance, one conditional move out of `pending`, `Mail.compose` (one `From`, fresh `Message-ID` on the alias's domain, the subject as one sanitized header line, the body UTF-8 in base64), `Transport.send` tagged `reply`, `SentRepo.remember`. A send that SES refuses or can't take leaves the draft pending; its `/outbox/<id>` page sends it again. Plain text only, subject ≤ 128 and body ≤ 20,000 characters (`MailSubject`/`MailText` in `@alias/contracts`, shared with MCP's `send_mail`). `ComposeRefused` (422) names `unknown-alias` (also another user's), `inactive-alias`, `invalid-address`, `blocked-contact` or `recipient-limit`; `SendFailed` gains `suppressed`.

**Send-to address** (the addy.io way, from the owner's own mail client). addy.io accepts `alias+recipient=example.com@domain`. We don't encode the recipient in clear: whoever passes the owner check could then mail anyone, and the owner check rests on SES's DKIM and DMARC verdicts, which can pass through SPF alignment on shared infrastructure (SR-11). Instead `POST /v1/aliases/:domain/:local/contacts { address }` (`contacts:write`) adds the address as a contact and returns its reverse alias as `replyAddress`; the web app shows it with "Copy send-to address". Mailing it is exactly a reply (§20.6): the token's 80-bit HMAC names the contact, the sender must be one of the owner's verified recipients with DKIM and DMARC `PASS` and a single `From` (SR-3), headers are stripped and quoted owner addresses scrubbed (§20.10). So knowing the alias and the owner's address is not enough; one also needs the unguessable send-to address, which only the owner sees. Adding the same address again returns the same contact and address and spends nothing. The API mints the token, so `ReverseKeys` moved from the inbound Lambda to `@alias/aws` (`ReverseKeys.config`, `ReverseKeys.dev`), and the API reads the same SSM parameter (`ALIAS_REVERSE_TOKEN_KEYS_PARAMETER`, `ssm:GetParameter` on its ARN).

**Limits** (`OutboxRepo`, the `Quota` helper, both engines):

| Allowance                                                                                                | Per user and UTC day                     | Past it                                                                         |
| -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Mail sent from aliases: replies, composed mail and drafts together (`SENDS#<date>`, was drafts only, 50) | 100 (`OutboxRepo.SENDS_PER_DAY`)         | compose/draft `SendFailed: limit-reached`; a reply is dropped as `send-limited` |
| New recipients: contacts the owner adds, by compose or send-to address (`NEW-RECIPIENTS#<date>`)         | 20 (`OutboxRepo.NEW_RECIPIENTS_PER_DAY`) | `recipient-limit`; known contacts still work                                    |

Sending to strangers is what spam needs, so new recipients have their own, smaller allowance; replying to people who wrote first does not, and shares the larger one. **Decision for the owner:** the numbers (100 and 20). Replies were uncapped per user before; a heavy user of reverse aliases now stops at 100 a day, silently (the reply is dropped and logged; a bounce back to the owner would be a new backscatter path).

**Suppression list.** Before a compose, a draft or a reply goes out, `Transport.isSuppressed` asks SESv2 `GetSuppressedDestination` (`NotFoundException` = not listed). A listed address is refused (`SendFailed: suppressed`, or `suppressed-recipient` for a reply) before the daily allowance is spent. AWS would accept such mail and drop it with an `OnAccountSuppressionList` bounce (§20.5); refusing says so to the owner instead. The check **fails open**: if SES can't answer (throttling, an outage), the mail goes out and SES still drops what it must. Verified on fakecloud (listed, unlisted, listed then removed, through the production helper); the API's and the inbound Lambda's roles get `ses:GetSuppressedDestination` (`Resource: "*"`, no resource-level permissions documented). AWS publishes no rate quota for it; if one bites, failing open keeps mail flowing.

**Blocklist** (§20.32). **Decision: the owner's blocklist works both ways.** It already drops mail from a listed address or domain and replies to it (the reply path runs the same filter, so a send-to address of a listed contact is dropped as `blocked-sender`); composing to such an address, adding it as a contact, and sending a draft to it are refused too (`ComposeRefused`/`InvalidContact`/`SendFailed: blocked-address`), the draft check at send time so a draft made before the entry doesn't go out. Rules (§20.32) describe incoming mail and don't apply to mail the owner writes.

**No token sends.** Compose stays session-only like the outbox's send (§20.24): a token, `mail:send` included, can at most draft to existing contacts through MCP. `contacts:write` may add a contact and read its send-to address, which only works from the owner's own DKIM-signed mailbox. MCP's `send_mail` still drafts to contacts only.

#### Failed deliveries

**What counts.** A forward to a forward-mode alias that fails for good after its delivery claim: `rejected` (SES refused the message), `recipient-bounced` (the forwarding address is paused, §20.28), `key-unusable` (its OpenPGP key no longer encrypts, §20.21) and `too-large` (over 25 MiB). Spam, viruses, loops, blocked contacts and caps are refusals, not failures, and stay as before; `both` aliases have the message in their inbox anyway; replies aren't forwards. Mail whose forwarding addresses are all paused used to be dropped before admission; it now goes through admission (loop check, hourly cap, contact) and the rewrite, so the kept copy is what the owner would have read, with a working reverse alias, and only the send is skipped. Outcomes and counters are unchanged. With several recipients per alias (§20.31) a failure is the message's, not a copy's: it is recorded when no copy goes out (every recipient paused, or every copy refused by SES or its key), with the reason `key-unusable` if any copy's key failed, else `rejected`; a message one recipient received is delivered, and a refused copy is only logged, as before. A rule's `forward-to` (§20.32) chooses the copy at receipt; a resend goes where the alias forwards now.

**What is stored** (`FailureRepo`, `Table.spec`, suite on fakecloud and DynamoDB Local): `FAILURES#<aliasId>` / `FAILURE#<id>` with the alias address, the sender's address (the `From` mailbox, else the envelope sender; a third party's address, like a contact), the reason, the size, when, and `expiresAt` a week later (TTL; readers ignore it from then on). Never the subject or body by default. Listed per user on gsi1 `FAILED-DELIVERIES#<userId>`, newest first. Written in one transaction with a `ConditionCheck` that the alias still exists, like stored mail, so deleting the alias (whose purge now takes the `FAILURES#` partition too) can't miss one. At most 100 a user per UTC day (`FAILURES#<date>`, `Limits.failuresPerDay`); past that, failures are only logged. Recording is best effort: the mail's outcome stands whatever happens.

**Kept copies, opt-in** (`isFailedMailKept` in `/v1/settings`, off by default). The rewritten forward, before encryption, up to the inbox's 10 MiB, sealed with the inbox envelope (`Envelope.sealBody`: a fresh data key wrapped by the SSM-held inbox keyring, AAD = alias id, failure id, part) and put under the 7-day class `inbox/7d/<aliasId>/<failureId>`, whose lifecycle rule already exists; the wrapped key, key version and the reverse alias it came from are in the failure item. `Inbox.purge` takes it with the alias or the account. Too-large mail is never kept. For an owner with an OpenPGP key the copy is sealed with the service's keys, not theirs (as inbox mail is): opting in means trusting the operator with a week of failed mail. **Decision for the owner:** whether to keep this opt-in per account (built) or per alias.

**API** (`FailuresGroup`, scopes `deliveries:read`/`deliveries:write`): `GET /v1/failed-deliveries`; `GET …/:id/message`, the kept copy as `message/rfc822` (`ResendFailed: not-kept` without one); `POST …/:id/resend`: the alias must still be the same and active; the copy goes to the mailboxes a forward of the alias goes to now, unpaused ones (`Forwarding.targetsOf` in `@alias/db`, the pipeline's own choice since §20.31/§20.34, moved out of `Policy` so both share it), each encrypted to its key if it has one (`Pgp.encrypt`, now also in the API Lambda), then `Transport.send` from the stored reverse alias, tagged `forward`, `SentRepo.remember` per recipient (its bounces are about that recipient, §20.28), the alias's forwarded counter; a copy that fails stops the resend (those already sent stay sent) and keeps the failure for another try; delivered, the failure and its copy are deleted. `DELETE …/:id`. Other users' failures answer `FailureNotFound`.

**Webhook** `mail.failed` (`{ aliasId, address, failureId, reason }`, no sender, keyed by SES message and recipient like `mail.blocked`). `key-unusable` and `too-large` still announce `mail.blocked` as before, so a subscriber to both sees both.

**Web app** (§20.16): "Send mail" in an alias's row menu (`?compose=<address>`), "Add contact" with the send-to address in the contacts sheet, a **Failed deliveries** screen (`/failed`) with Download, Resend and Delete, and "Keep failed mail for 7 days" in Settings. Accessible names: menu item and dialog "Send mail" (textboxes "To", "Subject", "Message", buttons "Send", "Cancel"), "Write" per contact, textbox "Contact address", buttons "Add contact" and "Copy send-to address", nav link and table "Failed deliveries" with "Download", "Resend", "Delete", switch "Keep failed mail for 7 days" (`aria-busy` until loaded). Browser tests (`browser/test/compose.test.ts`): compose arrives on fakecloud from the alias without the owner's mailbox or username; a contact added in the sheet gives a send-to address that, mailed over SMTP from the destination, leaves from the alias; with the switch on, a forward to a bounced destination is listed with reason and sender, Resend is refused until the destination works again, then delivers and the list empties. (The browser test re-adds the destination instead of Resume: the API allows one confirmation mail a minute.)

**Erasure** (§20.15): failures live in their alias's partition and copies under its prefix, both purged with each alias; the quota items are in the user's partition. Nothing new survives an erasure (`AccountRepo` suite with a kept failure and both new quotas: still only the tombstones and the journal).

**Tests.** Emulator (`isSuppressed` on fakecloud), repository on both engines (`FailureRepo`: per-user listing across aliases, expiry, owner-only delete, the alias check and purge, the daily cap under concurrency; `OutboxRepo.reserveRecipient`; erasure), `@alias/inbox` (kept copy sealed in the 7-day class, opens only under its own failure and alias, discard, purge), pipeline on fakecloud (a paused address recorded with metadata only, kept when opted in and the copy is the rewritten forward with its reverse alias, SES rejection recorded with a `mail.failed` event that names no sender, oversized mail without a copy, nothing for spam or `both` aliases, the daily cap; a first message to an added contact from the alias, a suppressed contact refused before the allowance, the daily send cap on replies), API handler (`compose.test.ts`: new address becomes a contact and the mail leaves from the alias naming nobody, refusals, suppression leaves the draft pending, both allowances, tokens get 403; contacts: send-to address verifies to the contact with the keys, idempotent, scopes; `failures.test.ts`: listing and isolation, download bytes and type, resend to the forwarding address then gone, refusals, delete and alias deletion take the copy, scopes), stack (`e2e`: compose → fakecloud SES shows `From` = the alias and the owner's mailbox nowhere; a contact added over the API, mailed from the owner's mailbox over SMTP, leaves as the alias, and a stranger's mail to it is dropped; a forward to a paused mailbox is listed with its sealed copy, downloaded, refused while paused, sent again after resuming and then gone from the table) and browser (compose, send-to address, failed deliveries with resend).

**Not verified (needs AWS).** `GetSuppressedDestination`'s real responses and rate quota, and its IAM; whether SES treats suppression-list addresses case-insensitively (we pass the stored, lowercased address); the 7-day lifecycle on kept copies (declared, §20.22.2).

### 20.34 Additional usernames (2026-10-23)

addy.io lets a user add usernames to keep, say, work and personal aliases apart. Until now one account had one username, which was both the login handle and the only subdomain (`<username>.<shared domain>`). Now the username chosen at sign-up stays the **primary** one (the login handle, never deleted), and an account may add up to **3 additional usernames**, each with its own subdomain of every shared domain, its own on-the-fly setting (§20.8) and its own default destination.

**Data** (`UsernameRepo` in `@alias/db`, `Table.ts`, suite on fakecloud and DynamoDB Local). An additional username claims its confusable skeleton with the same `USERNAME#<skeleton>` item sign-up writes, marked `isAdditional`, so sign-up and additional usernames share one namespace: neither can take a name, a lookalike or a tombstone of the other. The claim is written in one transaction with the user's `USERNAME#<username>` entry in their partition (the username's settings: `isOnTheFlyEnabled`, `defaultRecipientId`; listing is one strongly consistent Query) and a `USERNAMES` counter, so the cap is atomic (concurrent adds never pass it; a refused add claims nothing). `UsernameRepo.findOwner(username)` replaces `UserRepo.findByUsername`: exact username, primary or additional; a lookalike, a deleted or an erased username finds no one.

**Same squatting protections as sign-up** (§20.27). `POST /v1/usernames { username, turnstileToken }` runs `Username.parse` (length, characters, reserved names by skeleton), refuses a taken name (live, deleted or erased, lookalikes included), checks the cap, then redeems a Turnstile token and counts the new name as an account against the client's daily sign-up limit (5) and the hourly circuit breaker (100): a name and its subdomain are what a sign-up claims, so a script can't squat names faster by adding usernames than by signing up. Name and cap are checked before the token is redeemed, so a refusal never spends a solved widget. Adding is session-only (a token can't solve the widget, and the cookie path already has the same-origin check, §20.30); `GET` takes `usernames:read`, `PATCH` and `DELETE` take `usernames:write` (new scopes, also offered to OAuth clients, §20.24).

**API** (`UsernamesGroup` in `@alias/contracts`, `apps/api/src/usernames.ts`): `GET /v1/usernames` lists the primary (`isPrimary`, its on-the-fly setting from `/v1/settings`) and the additional ones with their subdomains, plus the `limit`. `PATCH /v1/usernames/:username { isOnTheFlyEnabled?, defaultRecipientId? }` changes one; `defaultRecipientId` must name one of the caller's verified destinations (`RecipientNotFound`, `RecipientNotVerified`), `null` goes back to the account default; on the primary only `isOnTheFlyEnabled` (the account setting) may change, a destination of its own is `PrimaryUsername` (409). `DELETE /v1/usernames/:username`; the primary is `PrimaryUsername`, someone else's or an unknown name `UsernameNotFound` (404). `GET /v1/aliases/domains` now lists every username's subdomains (primary first), and creating an alias on a subdomain checks the caller owns that exact username through its live claim (`not-your-subdomain` otherwise; role addresses stay refused on every subdomain). The MCP tools use the same handlers.

**Inbound** (`Pipeline`). On-the-fly creation on a user subdomain resolves the owner with `findOwner`: the primary's switch is the account setting, an additional username's is its own. Forwarding picks the destination with `Policy.forwardTargets(alias, recipients, preferred)`: the alias's own recipients if it names verified ones (§20.31), else the default destination of the additional username whose subdomain the alias is on, while it is one of the owner's verified destinations, else the account default (a removed or unconfirmed one falls back). That costs one more GetItem per forward on a user subdomain (none on shared and custom domains).

**Deleting an additional username** (§20.15), resumable: (1) its claim becomes a tombstone (`deletedAt`, no owner), so nothing new is created on its subdomains, by the API or on the fly, and nobody, the owner included, ever gets the name or a lookalike again; (2) every alias on its subdomains is deleted like an alias (contacts, counters, stored mail in S3, an `alias.deleted` webhook each), and the alias tombstones on those subdomains go too (`AliasRepo.releaseDomain`), since the username's tombstone covers the whole subdomain, as for an erased account's subdomain; (3) the entry and a counter slot go in one transaction. A failure leaves the username listed and a retry finishes. The web dialog says the name can't be taken again.

**Erasure** (§20.15). `AccountRepo.Account` names the additional usernames; step 2 turns every claim into a tombstone, aliases on all the user's subdomains leave no tombstone, and the entries and counter go with the partition in step 4. The table holds one more bare tombstone per additional username, keyed by its skeleton.

**SES identities.** None needed in production: the shared domain's identity covers every subdomain (§20.5). Dev's hook (`apps/api/bin/dev.ts`) now also verifies `<username>.<shared domain>` on fakecloud after a successful `POST /v1/usernames`; tests verify the extra subdomain explicitly, as they do the primary's.

**Web** (§20.16). Domains screen: a "Usernames" card (table "Usernames": username with a "Primary" badge, subdomains, a destination select "Destination for <username>" with "Account default" and the verified destinations, the switch "Create aliases on the fly on <username>", "Delete <username>"), "Add username" (dialog "Add a username" with the textbox "Username", the Turnstile widget and "Add username"; `?dialog=add-username`), and "Delete username?" (`?deleteUsername=<name>`). Aliases screen: a "Filter by username" select (`?username=`) once the account has more than one username; the create dialog lists every subdomain under "Your subdomains, any address". The aliases screen's "Create aliases on the fly" switch stays the primary's.

**Tests.** Repository on both engines (`usernameRepoSuite.ts`: exact owner vs lookalike, shared namespace with sign-up both ways, two users adding one name at once, the cap under five concurrent adds with nothing claimed past it, partial updates and clearing, deletion in two resumable steps that frees the slot and keeps the name taken for everyone, never another user's or a primary; `accountRepoSuite.ts`: the "one of everything" user now has an additional username with settings and aliases on its subdomains, and erasure leaves exactly one more bare tombstone). Unit (`forwardTarget` with a preferred, unconfirmed or removed destination). Pipeline on fakecloud (the username's own on-the-fly switch and destination, the primary unaffected, nothing created once it is being deleted). API (`usernames.test.ts`: list and alias creation on the new subdomains, name rules shared with sign-up, the human check after name and cap, the cap and the sign-up limits and circuit breaker, settings and destination checks, the primary's rules, deletion with aliases, contacts, tombstones and webhook events, token scopes, erasure). Browser (`browser/test/usernames.test.ts`: add through the emulated widget, switch, create an alias on the new subdomain, filter by username, delete). Stack (`e2e`: SMTP to a new address on the additional subdomain is created on the fly and forwarded to its own destination, the primary's alias to the account default; after deletion the mail is dropped and the table holds nothing naming the subdomain but delivery claims).

**Not verified / open.** As with erasure (§20.3), an alias created on the fly an instant before step 1 may be missed by the eventually consistent alias index in step 2; it stays the owner's, listed and deletable, and keeps forwarding. No CLI commands for usernames yet (the SDK has the endpoints). Decisions for the owner: the cap of 3; whether adding a username should also cost a fresh passkey assertion; whether deleting one should let its owner take it again later (today: never, like an erased username).

### 20.35 Browser extension (2026-10-23)

Like addy.io's extension: make an alias right in a sign-up form. `apps/extension` is one Manifest V3 codebase for Chromium and Firefox, built with Vite (`build.ts`), on `@alias/sdk`.

**What it does.** The popup (React, TanStack Query/Form like the web app) offers, for the open tab, **"Alias for example.co.uk: `example@ada.alias.example`"** (the local part is the site's registrable domain without its public suffix, by the Public Suffix List with private suffixes, `tldts`; `Site.ts`) and **"New random alias"** (first shared domain). Either is created, filled into the focused field (or the first empty email field) and copied; an alias the user already has for that site is handed out again (aliases on their own subdomain are theirs, so `AliasTaken` means "you made it"). Below: copy and fill buttons and the five newest aliases. On any editable field the context menu has **Alias → Fill an alias for this site / Fill a new random alias**, which creates and fills in the right-clicked field, in its frame. With more than one username (§20.34) a **Username** select under the button picks the subdomain (the primary one's first, which is also what the context menu uses). Every alias made here records the site in its description (§20.31): `example.co.uk (browser extension)`.

**One codebase, two browsers** (`Manifest.ts`). Chromium runs the background as a service worker (`background.service_worker`), Firefox as an event page (`background.scripts`, it has no extension service workers); both as ES modules. Firefox also gets `browser_specific_settings.gecko`: a fixed add-on ID (a UUID, which claims no domain; it must never change once published), `strict_min_version` 140 (built-in data-collection consent) and `data_collection_permissions.required: ["authenticationInfo", "browsingActivity"]`: the sign-in, and the site's domain when the user asks for an alias named after it, both sent only to the Alias server they signed in to. Both browsers implement promise-returning `chrome.*` in MV3, so there is no polyfill. `pnpm extension:package` (`scripts/package-extension.ts`) builds both and writes `apps/extension/dist/alias-<target>-<version>.zip`, reproducibly (sorted entries, 1980 timestamps; two builds give the same bytes); CI's `extension` job uploads them. The bundle isn't minified, so store reviewers read what runs.

**Permissions, and why no more.** `activeTab` (the open tab's address and the right to fill a field in it, only after a click on the toolbar button or the context menu, for that tab), `scripting` (to run the fill function then), `contextMenus`, `identity` (`launchWebAuthFlow`), `storage`. Host permission only for the server: the build's (`ALIAS_EXTENSION_SERVER`, granted at install) or, for self-hosters, the one typed at sign-in, asked for then from `optional_host_permissions` (`https://*/*`, loopback). No content script, no `<all_urls>`, no `web_accessible_resources`, no `externally_connectable`: nothing runs in a page until the user clicks, and no page can message the extension. Host permissions bypass CORS for extension pages, so the API needs no CORS headers.

**Sign-in: OAuth 2.1 with PKCE through `identity.launchWebAuthFlow`, against our authorization server (§20.24).** Chosen over the device flow: one window with the passkey login and the consent card instead of a code to compare, and no polling from a background that Chromium may stop after 30 idle seconds. `CodeLogin` (new in `@alias/sdk`, for any client that can catch a redirect) does the protocol: RFC 8414 metadata (issuer and authorization endpoint), RFC 7591 registration, a fresh verifier and `state` per sign-in (32 random bytes each, Web Crypto), `S256`, then on the way back checks the redirect target, `state` and `iss` (RFC 9207) before redeeming the code; refresh and RFC 7009 revocation. The extension registers a client at each sign-in with its own redirect URI (`identity.getRedirectURL()`: `https://<extension id>.chromiumapp.org/`, `https://<hash>.extensions.allizom.org/` in Firefox), so it works on any server with no configuration there and with unpacked builds; only the extension with that ID receives that redirect, which is what binds the code to it. Clients nobody used expire after 30 days (§20.30). Scopes: `aliases:read aliases:write`, nothing else.

**API-bound access tokens (server change).** Until now every OAuth token was bound to `<origin>/mcp`, and the HTTP API took only personal tokens. The authorization server now also issues tokens for a second resource, **`<origin>/v1`** (RFC 8707 `resource`; none named still means the MCP server), and the API's `Authentication` accepts an `alias_oat_` access token **only if it is bound to `<origin>/v1`**, of a grant that still exists and a user who still exists; scope checks are the personal tokens' (a token never reaches session-only endpoints: tokens, webhooks, consent, device approval, outbox sending), failures count against the same per-address limit. The MCP endpoint keeps refusing anything not bound to it, so a token for one resource is useless at the other. The consent card says "through the Alias API" instead of "through MCP" for these. Access tokens live an hour, refresh tokens 30 days and rotate; disconnecting the app under Settings → Connected apps ends it at once.

**Where the tokens live.** The refresh token (and server, client ID, username) in `storage.local`, so a browser restart doesn't sign the user out; the access token in `storage.session`, in memory only, gone when the browser closes. Neither area is reachable from web pages, and no content script of ours sits in one. Only the background holds them and talks to the server; it refreshes under one lock (`Semaphore`), since two concurrent refreshes with one token look like theft to the server, which revokes the grant. A refused refresh (`invalid_grant`: revoked, disconnected, lapsed) signs out. Sign-out revokes the refresh token (RFC 7009 takes the grant and its access tokens) and forgets everything even if the server is unreachable. Requests go with `credentials: "omit"`: a web-app session cookie would make the API treat them as the web app's and refuse them as cross-site, and the extension must act with exactly its token's scopes.

**Message passing.** The popup asks the background through `runtime.sendMessage`; `Messages.ts` has both directions as Effect Schemas (`Request`, `Response` tagged unions). The background answers only its own popup (`sender.id` and `sender.url`); each side encodes what it sends and decodes what it gets (so `Option` fields cross as plain JSON), and words every failure as a sentence (a defect still answers, so the popup never hangs). The fill function is the only code that enters a page: injected with `scripting.executeScript` into the clicked frame's isolated world, self-contained, it sets the value through the element's own setter and fires `input` and `change` (so React and Vue see typing), reads nothing and returns a boolean.

**CSP and remote code.** Extension pages run under `script-src 'self'; object-src 'none'; base-uri 'none'; form-action 'none'; frame-ancestors 'none'`; everything is bundled, no remote scripts, no `eval` (checked in the bundle). `connect-src` is left to host permissions so a self-hoster's server works without a rebuild.

**Tests.** Unit (`apps/extension/test`): registrable domains and local parts (multi-label suffixes, private suffixes, IDN, IPs, `localhost`, other schemes), server origins (https only except loopback, no credentials), the manifest per browser (background kind, Firefox settings, permissions, no content scripts, the CSP). API handler (`apps/api/test/oauth.test.ts`): a `<origin>/v1` token calls the API with its scopes, gets `Forbidden` for others and for session-only endpoints, 401 at `/mcp`; refresh keeps the resource; disconnecting ends it; an MCP-bound token gets 401 from the API. SDK (`packages/sdk/test/codeLogin.test.ts`, the API on the function URL emulator): the whole `CodeLogin` flow, the token used through `AliasClient`, refresh, logout; a forged `state`, a foreign `iss`, a foreign redirect target and a refusal fail, and none of them spends the code. **Browser** (`browser/test/extension.test.ts`): the built extension loaded unpacked into Chromium in its **new headless mode** (the full browser with `channel: "chromium"` and a persistent profile; the headless shell can't load extensions, verified), against the emulated stack: Ada signs up, clicks "Sign in" in the popup, the `launchWebAuthFlow` window shows the consent card ("through the Alias API", the extension's name unverified), she allows it; on a sign-up page at `shop.example.co.uk` (Chromium's host resolver maps it to a port-0 server) the popup creates `example@ada.alias.test` and fills it (the page's own `input` listener sees it), hands it out again the second time, fills a random one, then `example@ada-work.alias.test` on her second username picked in the select; all three carry the site in their description; sign-out leaves no connected app; no CSP violation. The test grants the sign-up site at install, because Playwright can't click the toolbar button or a context menu, the gestures that grant `activeTab`.

**Not verified.** The context menu (Playwright can't open native menus; it calls the same `createAndFill` as the popup with the clicked frame). Firefox: the build is checked by the manifest test only, not loaded in a Firefox (Playwright's Firefox can't load extensions); `web-ext lint` isn't run. `chrome.action.openPopup()` after a failed context-menu fill depends on the browser version.

**Publishing (not done; needs the maintainers' accounts).** Chrome Web Store: a developer account (one-time US$5 fee, 2-step verification), the listing (description, screenshots, 128 px icon, privacy-policy URL), the privacy practices form (single purpose, a justification per permission, data use: authentication information and website content/activity sent to the user's Alias server), then upload the Chromium zip; the store signs it. addons.mozilla.org: a Mozilla account with 2FA, upload the Firefox zip (listed, or unlisted for self-distribution; either way AMO signs it), and, since the bundle is built, the source with build instructions (`pnpm install && pnpm extension:package`; the zips are reproducible). Both need the official server's origin for the default build (`ALIAS_EXTENSION_SERVER`) once one is deployed; until then the user types theirs.

### 20.37 Deploying a stage: what is automated, and the runbook (2026-10-23)

The first production deploy used to take a page of manual steps (`infra/alchemy.run.ts`'s comments, §20.26's list): five `aws ssm put-parameter` commands, a Turnstile widget made in the dashboard and its keys copied into SSM and the environment, the ACM validation CNAME copied into Cloudflare, a second deploy with `ALIAS_APP_CERTIFICATE_ISSUED=true`, the DNS records for mail and the app, a Service Quotas request. Now one command does all of it: **`pnpm infra:deploy`** (`scripts/deploy.ts`). Infra stays typecheck-only; no app code changed and no runtime AWS dependency was added.

**What the deploy does** (Alchemy `2.0.0-beta.83`, APIs read in `node_modules/alchemy/src`):

| Step                                                                                                   | How                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                    |
| ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| AWS credentials                                                                                        | Alchemy's own: its profile (`pnpm infra:alchemy profile edit` → AWS → SSO, naming the profile `aws configure sso` made). For an SSO profile Alchemy starts `aws sso login` itself, which is why the AWS CLI stays (from `mise.deploy.toml`, `mise -E deploy`). The stack's own AWS calls (the actions below, the origin secret's version) use `AWSEnvironment`'s credentials, so no keys are exported anywhere                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                         |
| Web build                                                                                              | `pnpm --filter @alias/web build`, uploaded by `Website.AssetDeployment` (§20.26)                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                       |
| Keyrings `/alias/<stage>/{reverse-token-keys,image-proxy-keys,inbox-keys,webhook-keys,origin-secrets}` | The `Keyring` action, one per keyring: `PutParameter` without `Overwrite`, `SecureString`, `1:<base64url of 32 random bytes>`. SSM refuses an existing name, so reruns and hand-rotated keys are safe. The value is made inside the action and returned nowhere: the state holds the name only (**the keys stay out of the Alchemy state**, the owner's decision). The functions take the parameter names from the actions' Outputs, so none starts before its keys exist. A parameter deleted later comes back only with `--force` (actions run again only when their input changes). `OriginSecretHeader` then reads `origin-secrets` for the distribution's header (that value was always in the state, §20.26); its input carries the parameter's version read at plan time, so a rotation's deploy reads it again |
| Turnstile widget                                                                                       | `Cloudflare.Turnstile.Widget` (`domains: [app domain]`, `mode: "managed"`); its site key into the API's `ALIAS_TURNSTILE_SITE_KEY`, its secret into `AWS.SSM.Parameter` `/alias/<stage>/turnstile-secret` (`SecureString`). The secret is in the state: Cloudflare generates it and returns it to any reader of the widget, so the state holds nothing Cloudflare doesn't. The `local` stage gets the always-passing test keys                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                         |
| Zones                                                                                                  | Looked up by name at plan time (`Cloudflare.Zone.resolveZoneId`: the domain or its closest parent zone in `CLOUDFLARE_ACCOUNT_ID`); a domain without one stops the deploy before anything changes                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                      |
| Certificate                                                                                            | `AWS.ACM.Certificate` waits for issuance only through Route 53, so two Alchemy `Action`s do it for Cloudflare: `CertificateValidationRecord` polls ACM until the validation record exists, `Cloudflare.DNS.Record` writes it, `CertificateIssued` polls until `ISSUED` (every 10 s, 15 minutes at most; a terminal status fails). The distribution takes the ARN from that action, so it gets the alternate domain only once the certificate is usable. Actions run again only when their input changes: later deploys don't wait                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                      |
| DNS (all DNS only)                                                                                     | Per shared and temp domain: the three Easy DKIM CNAMEs `<token>._domainkey.<domain>` → `<token>.dkim.amazonses.com` from `SES.EmailIdentity`'s `dkimTokens`, MX `10 inbound-smtp.eu-west-1.amazonaws.com`; per shared domain also `*.<domain>` MX (users' subdomains, §20.8); the app domain CNAME → the distribution (flattened at the apex)                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                          |
| Lambda concurrency                                                                                     | `AWS.ServiceQuotas.ServiceQuotaIncreaseRequest` (`lambda`, `L-B99A9384`, 1,000); submitted only while the applied quota is lower and no request is open                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                |
| Budget                                                                                                 | `AWS.Budgets.Budget` `alias-<stage>-monthly`, `ALIAS_BUDGET_USD` (default 20) a month; mail to `ALIAS_ALERT_EMAIL` at 80% actual and 100% forecast                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                     |
| `alchemy deploy --stage <ALIAS_STAGE>`                                                                 | In `infra/`; extra flags pass through (`--yes`, `--adopt`)                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                             |

Every setting is in **`infra/.env.example`**; copy it to `infra/.env` (ignored by git), which both `scripts/deploy.ts` and Alchemy read. `scripts/deploy.ts` only builds the web app and runs `alchemy deploy --stage $ALIAS_STAGE`.

**Not automated, and why.**

- **SPF and DMARC for the shared domains.** No policy is decided here: the shared domains send with SES's default MAIL FROM (`amazonses.com`), so SPF isn't what aligns (Easy DKIM is), and §19.3 only says not to force `p=reject` onto _users'_ domains. A `_dmarc` record for the shared and temp domains (and whether to add a custom MAIL FROM, `bounce.<domain>`, as custom domains get, §20.18) is a decision for the owner; once made, it is two more `Cloudflare.DNS.Record`s.
- **SES production access.** A human at AWS reviews the request (use case, volumes, bounce handling). Alchemy's `SES.AccountSettings` doesn't model it; the SESv2 `PutAccountDetails` call exists but only files the same request, so it stays a console step.
- **CloudFront flat-rate plan.** A billing commitment (§20.26); the PricingPlanManager API is in `@distilled.cloud/aws`, but Alchemy has no resource for it.
- **Waiting for the concurrency quota.** The request isn't waited for (approval can take from minutes to days). On an account that starts below 452, the first deploy fails at the reserved functions; deploy again once Service Quotas shows the request approved. Alchemy picks up where it stopped.
- **The AWS account, SSO, and the Cloudflare token.** Credentials are made by people.

**Runbook: the first deploy of a stage.**

1. **AWS account** (AWS Organizations member or standalone, **not** on the AWS Free Tier plan: such accounts can't subscribe to a flat-rate plan, §20.26). Enable IAM Identity Center, and create a user with the `AdministratorAccess` permission set on the account. Then `pnpm infra:aws configure sso` (profile name `alias`, region `eu-west-1`) and `pnpm infra:alchemy profile edit` (AWS → SSO → profile `alias`; Alchemy runs the browser login itself). `pnpm infra:aws` is the AWS CLI v2 from `mise.deploy.toml` (pinned, checksums in `mise.deploy.lock`); mise installs it on first use, only for this, `pnpm infra:alchemy` and `pnpm infra:deploy` (`mise -E deploy`), so `pnpm agent:setup` and CI never download it.
2. **Cloudflare.** Every shared and temp domain must be a zone (or under one) in one account, with Cloudflare's nameservers active at the registrar. Create an API token (My Profile → API Tokens → Create custom token) with **Account · Turnstile · Edit**, **Zone · Zone · Read** and **Zone · DNS · Edit** for those zones. Note the account ID (dashboard sidebar).
3. **Settings.** `cp infra/.env.example infra/.env` and fill in `ALIAS_STAGE`, `ALIAS_SHARED_DOMAINS`, `ALIAS_TEMP_DOMAINS`, `ALIAS_ALERT_EMAIL`, `CLOUDFLARE_ACCOUNT_ID`, `CLOUDFLARE_API_TOKEN` (and `ALCHEMY_PROFILE` if not `default`).
4. **Deploy:** `pnpm infra:deploy`. If a domain already has records under the same names (an old MX, a parked CNAME), Alchemy refuses to take them over; delete them in Cloudflare or rerun with `pnpm infra:deploy --adopt`. If the Lambda quota is still pending (step above), wait for Service Quotas → AWS Lambda → "Concurrent executions" to show 1,000 and run it again.
5. **Confirm the alert mail:** follow the link in the "AWS Notification - Subscription Confirmation" mail to `ALIAS_ALERT_EMAIL`.
6. **SES production access** (new accounts are in the sandbox: 200 mails a day, verified recipients only): SES console (eu-west-1) → Account dashboard → Request production access. Mail type transactional; describe forwarding to users' own verified addresses, bounce and complaint handling (§20.11, §20.28) and the reputation alarms (§20.13). Until it's granted, forwarding reaches only verified addresses.
7. **CloudFront flat-rate plan:** CloudFront console → the distribution `alias (<stage>)` → Pricing plan → Free (§20.26). Optional: without it the distribution is billed pay-as-you-go (≈ $9 a month for the WAF at no traffic).
8. **Check** what §20.26 ("What a deploy must verify") and §20.29 list, and that `https://<app domain>` serves the app with a working sign-up (the Turnstile widget is the real one now).

Every later deploy is `pnpm infra:deploy`; when the SSO session has expired, Alchemy says so and `pnpm infra:alchemy profile refresh` logs in again.
